How to Support Someone With Postpartum Depression
2026-09-04

How to Support Someone With Postpartum Depression

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When Iza Thiago-Munoz brought her first baby home, she instituted a rule: She was the only person allowed to carry the baby upstairs. At the time, she lived in a narrow Philadelphia rowhouse with a steep staircase. If anyone else—including her husband—wanted to take the baby up, they had to buckle the infant into a car seat first. Her husband occasionally tried to reason with her. He could hold the baby tightly, he pointed out. “But what if you fall down the stairs?” she would reply. She didn’t know it at the time, but Thiago-Munoz was struggling with postpartum depression and anxiety, which amplified her lifelong tendency to imagine the worst-case scenario. She also felt enormous pressure to be a perfect mother. “I thought I had to do everything,” she says. Otherwise, she worried, “people would just think that I wasn’t good enough.” Asking for help felt like admitting that they were right. Thiago-Munoz is now a lactation consultant, postpartum doula, and founder of Clementina Health, a postpartum and lactation telehealth company. Her work is informed by what she experienced after giving birth—and by what she wishes the people around her had understood. “I just didn’t feel joy in anything,” she says. “I felt like I was supposed to be happy because, wow, this major thing just happened. And I was happy, but I wasn’t joyful.” Supporting someone with postpartum depression starts with knowing how it can show up, even when it doesn’t match the image you have in your head. Both the “baby blues” and postpartum depression can cause sadness, tearfulness, irritability, and feeling overwhelmed after giving birth, says Dr. Kelli Burroughs, an ob-gyn and vice chair of clinical affairs and patient experience at the University of Texas Medical School in Houston. But the baby blues are generally mild, begin within the first week after delivery, and improve within about two weeks. Postpartum depression lasts longer and interferes more significantly with daily life. Signs can include a persistently depressed mood; losing interest in things that usually bring joy; withdrawing from other people; difficulty eating, concentrating, or sleeping even when the baby is asleep; extreme fatigue; and feeling disconnected from the baby. Postpartum depression can also begin months after giving birth—not only during those first blurry weeks at home. It doesn’t always look the way people expect, either. Sometimes, the moms who are suffering the most appear to have everything under control, says Jayme Scarfo, a licensed professional counselor in Surprise, Ariz., who specializes in working with pregnant and postpartum women. They’re showering, cooking, going to the gym, and insisting: “It’s hard, but don’t worry. I’ve got it.” You don’t need to decide whether someone meets the diagnostic criteria before checking in. Describe what you’ve noticed without labeling her, Scarfo suggests: “I know this is a huge adjustment, and you haven’t seemed like yourself lately. How are you really doing?” Or: “You seem much more anxious than usual, and I’m worried about you. Can we talk about it?” Consider who should initiate that conversation, too. “The best person to ask those hard questions is often going to be whoever she feels the least pressure to perform for,” Scarfo says. That might be a sister, best friend, therapist, or fellow mom—not necessarily her partner or mother. Burroughs also likes asking questions that are harder to brush off with a reflexive “I’m fine.” For example: “Are you having more good days or bad days?” It’s also helpful to ask when she last slept for more than two hours or ate a real meal sitting down. Those answers can help you understand where she’s struggling—and how you might lighten the load. When someone declares they’re a “terrible mother,” your instinct might be to disagree. Of course you’re not! You’re doing an amazing job. But depression and anxiety symptoms can start to feel like part of someone’s identity, says Emma Basch, a clinical psychologist in Washington who specializes in perinatal mental health. Trying to prove her wrong might leave her feeling even more misunderstood. Instead of correcting her, acknowledge how much she’s hurting: “It must be so painful to feel that way. I’m here for you.” Burroughs likes: “I’m glad you told me. I’m glad you feel comfortable confiding in me.” That signals that you can handle the truth, and that your friend doesn’t need to resume pretending everything is fine. Skip the sunny reassurances, like “Enjoy every moment,” “They’re only this little once,” and especially “You just had a baby—you should be happy.” Those comments suggest she can simply choose her way out of what she’s experiencing. “Postpartum depression is a true medical condition,” Burroughs says. “This is not a sign of weakness. This is out of the control of the new mom.” Loved ones should try to remain calm if a new mom shares an intrusive thought, Scarfo says. A frightening flash like “What if I drop the baby?” isn’t the same as wanting it to happen. An alarmed reaction—or immediately suggesting an intervention like hospitalization—could make her less likely to confide in you again. Acknowledge that the thought sounds scary, and gently ask more: “How often are you having these thoughts?” “Do you feel like you might act on them?” “Do you feel safe right now?” “Who can we call together?” You don’t need to act like the thought is no big deal; it clearly feels like a very big deal to her. The goal is to become a safe place for her to say the things that aren’t picture-perfect, Scarfo says—and then help her figure out what she needs next. Talking about suicide or harming the baby does require immediate intervention, Basch says. The same is true of signs of postpartum psychosis, which can include hallucinations, delusions, paranoia, confusion, or a decreased need for sleep accompanied by a surge of energy. “It’s not a layperson’s job to diagnose,” she says, “but it’s a layperson’s job to take action.” Anyone experiencing a mental-health crisis can call or text 988. The National Maternal Mental Health Hotline, available at 1-833-TLC-MAMA, also provides free, confidential support 24/7 to pregnant and postpartum women and their families. “Let me know if you need anything” might sound kind. But it puts the burden back on the person who’s already overwhelmed. She hears “ball’s in your court,” Scarfo says, and “a depressed mom is not picking up the ball. She has 17 other things to do.” Instead, tell your loved one exactly what you’d like to do, Basch advises. “I’m dropping off dinner tonight—does 6 p.m. work?” is much easier to answer than “Can I bring you something sometime?” She can still say no or suggest another time, but she doesn’t have to identify a need, invent a solution, and delegate it to you. Be careful not to turn helping into unwanted visits. Scarfo and her sister-in-law used to leave meals on each other’s porches and say: “I’m dropping off dinner at 6. I’m going to leave it on the porch, and I’ll text you when it’s there.” There was no expectation that the recipient would invite anyone inside or make conversation. “It doesn’t put that burden of gratitude on the new mom,” Scarfo says—“that burden of performing or hosting.” Professional treatment might include therapy, medication, or both. Yet finding a provider, staying on top of insurance, making calls, and arranging child care can feel impossible when someone is already overwhelmed. Basch has seen loved ones do that legwork themselves and say: “I found a few therapists who specialize in postpartum mental health. I’d be happy to help you connect with one.” You might offer to make the call, accompany your loved one to an appointment, or watch the baby while she goes. If your partner keeps insisting that she’s fine but you remain concerned, Burroughs suggests contacting her ob-gyn, midwife, or other maternal-care provider yourself. Be specific about what you’ve observed: persistent sadness, repeated crying, withdrawal, difficulty bonding with the baby, or struggling to complete essential tasks. You might say: “I’m concerned about my partner’s emotional recovery after giving birth. These are the changes I’ve noticed at home.” The provider can then reach out to the patient without necessarily revealing who called, Burroughs says. They’ll usually open by saying: “I’m just calling to check on you. I want to see how your recovery is going.” Then, they’ll ask how the new mom feels emotionally on a scale of 1 to 10, whether she’s bonding with the baby, how often she feels sad, and whether she’s having more good days or bad ones. Those specific questions can make it harder for serious symptoms to disappear behind “I’m fine.” Support tends to pour in immediately after a baby is born, when everyone wants to deliver a casserole and tickle the newborn’s toes. Then people return to their own lives, even though the need for support often outlasts that initial flurry of attention. Continue checking in without demanding a response: “Thinking of you today. No need to text back.” Keep offering specific help, even if your loved one turns you down the first time. Scarfo suggests making it clear that you’ll come over whether she wants to run an errand alone or lie down on the couch while somebody else handles the chaos around her. Thiago-Munoz still remembers the neighbor who noticed that she had stopped taking her dog on his usual 9 a.m. walk. If Thiago-Munoz hadn’t appeared by 9:15, her neighbor came over and walked him herself. “I honestly never would have asked her to take the dog for a walk,” she says. “I didn’t have the capacity to look for someone to do it. She knew my routine.”

AI Will Transform Work, But It Can't Replace Relationships
2026-09-04

AI Will Transform Work, But It Can't Replace Relationships

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings AI is everywhere. It's reshaping industries, accelerating innovation, and changing how millions of people work. And while AI will transform work, it is crystal clear to me that AI can not replace relationships. Working in real estate has taught me this firsthand. Great real estate agents are more than just salespeople. Real estate agents help people as they build, and often rebuild, their lives. They act as crisis managers, trusted confidants during divorce, empty-nester therapists, grief counselors, life coaches, matchmakers, and above all, trusted advisors who guide clients through some of life's biggest transitions. This is not to say that the real estate sector is beyond disruption. In fact, it is a particularly turbulent industry. And yet, the role of real estate agents has survived global pandemics, market ups and downs, shifting mortgage rates, and every other major industry disruption. AI will be no different for real estate agents, or other relationship-based professions. To be sure, AI is reshaping how we all work. And many workers are understandably anxious. Relentless reports predicting job loss and economic disruption have perhaps unsurprisingly deepened public skepticism about AI, particularly among young adults and recent college graduates. Numerous commencement speakers who touted AI this graduation season were booed, including both Google’s former and current CEOs.Communities across the country are pushing back against the rapid expansion of AI data centers, raising legitimate concerns about rising energy demands, water consumption, and the environmental impact these massive facilities can have on the places they call home. Even Pope Leo XIV weighed in on the rise of AI, publishing his first encyclical letter, Magnifica Humanitas, which thoughtfully evaluates the future of humanity in the age of AI. All of these concerns have exacerbated the AI trust gap. However, once a transformative technology is unleashed on society, there's no putting the genie back in the bottle. When Netflix sunsetted DVDs and made it possible for people to stream movies from the comfort of their homes, people stopped going to Blockbuster. When Spotify put a million songs in our pockets, people stopped buying CDs. AI is also a service that can make people’s lives easier. For example, it's really good at writing code. In my own industry, AI has dramatically lowered the barrier to innovation by empowering real estate professionals to build software and workflows tailored to their unique business needs—something expensive, off-the-shelf software often fails to deliver. Before AI, if a real estate professional had an idea for a new tool or workflow, they typically needed to hire a software developer, secure funding, and wait months—sometimes even years—for a finished product. Today, that equation has changed. With AI and vibe coding, they can turn an idea into a working prototype in a matter of hours with a fraction of the financial investment once required. I have a colleague who owns a real estate brokerage that now builds all of its technology in-house. Using Claude, he has created dynamic market reports, interactive sales dashboards, and even a custom Gmail widget that allows his agents to access and update the company's CRM without ever leaving their inbox. There's no need to log into another application or juggle multiple browser tabs. This kind of change isn't unique to real estate. It’s happening in education, finance, manufacturing, and virtually every other industry. No matter your profession, you may spend tremendous amounts of time on administrative work: writing spreadsheet formulas, analyzing documents, sending email, producing marketing content, organizing information, preparing presentations, and tackling repetitive tasks that add little strategic value. But whether you're an attorney, consultant, plumber, financial advisor, or sales executive, your greatest value doesn’t come from routine administrative work. It comes from solving problems and building relationships. Tools like ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini can analyze contracts, create reports, summarize research, review documents, and automate a plethora of other tedious tasks. They allow professionals to spend less time on administration and more time serving their clients and building meaningful relationships. But AI isn't replacing the qualities that matter most. AI can't demonstrate genuine empathy, navigate difficult conversations, inspire confidence, or build lasting relationships. It can't sit across the table from a family deciding whether to move across the country or coach a struggling employee. It can't read the room, recognize when someone is hesitant, or understand the deeply personal circumstances behind many of life's biggest decisions. Those are uniquely human capabilities and always will be. As AI becomes better at technical tasks, human skills become the differentiator. Communication, empathy, and trust will define the next generation of successful professionals. The future of work isn't choosing between people and AI. It's using AI to amplify the human experience. Because AI will transform work, but it can't replace relationship TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. What Tech Titans Don’t Understand About Sci-Fi
2026-09-04

What Tech Titans Don’t Understand About Sci-Fi

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of the Books Briefing, our editors’ weekly guide to the best in books. Sign up for it here. When I read Thomas More’s Utopia in college, the main thing I retained was that the inhabitants of More’s imaginary island worked just six hours a day and earned what we might now call a universal basic income. But the fact that I came away from this 16th-century classic with only visions of a shorter workday and a generous allowance says more about my undergraduate habits than it does about More’s ideas or intentions. Looking back recently, I remembered that his civilization wasn’t exactly the campus commune of my dreams. Its citizens held slaves, some of whom were shackled for committing adultery. The punishment for premarital sex, meanwhile, was permanent celibacy; the whole place sounded like a punitive surveillance state. I’d somehow missed all of this initially. Still, as Gal Beckerman noted this week in an Atlantic essay about Silicon Valley’s misreadings of science fiction, I wasn’t the first person to make this type of error. Fortunately, my myopia was harmless. Not all misunderstandings are. First, here are five new stories from The Atlantic Books Section: In her new book, The Rise and Fall of the Artificial State, the historian Jill Lepore close-reads the science-fiction books that proved formative for Elon Musk, Sam Altman, and Peter Thiel. In their early-teen years, these future billionaires devoured the works of Robert Heinlein, Isaac Asimov, Arthur C. Clarke, and other dark fantasists—but they seem to still hold on to the blinkered interpretations from their youth. Their collective misunderstandings fascinated Beckerman. “What they seemed not to get,” he writes of these men, “was that these were cautionary tales, stories that projected deep societal worries into space or onto a future many millennia away.” Instead of seeing the dysfunction behind these invented societies, “these boys just thought the killer robots sounded cool.” Lepore argues that these seeming misreads are partly responsible for their obsessions with technological progress—no matter the human cost. Three centuries after More coined the word utopia (which sounds in Greek like “good place” but actually means “no place”), the philosopher John Stuart Mill effectively popularized its opposite, dystopia, to describe worlds in which the worst things come to pass. In the 20th century, dystopia became a key feature of science fiction. Some of my favorite works in the genre introduce us to societies that seem idyllic, before revealing the rot within these supposedly perfect worlds. They aim, I think, to show that any utopia is probably too good to be true. It’s no place—or worse. More’s Utopia isn’t all that different; it depicts a society that readers might not actually want to live in. More, an English Catholic leader who was executed by King Henry VIII, probably didn’t mean to advocate for an essentially communist society with legalized euthanasia. Critics have long speculated that his depiction was satirical—that the first utopian literary work was, at least in part, dystopian. This wouldn’t surprise me, because years of living and reading have conditioned me to accept that every paradise has a dark side. This is why I was dismayed to hear Sam Altman say, earlier this year, that an AI-driven process to choose the U.S. president could be “awesome.” Seventy years ago, in his short story “Franchise,” Asimov imagined one variation on this scenario: A computer picks one median American to “vote” for president. In the story, democracy becomes nothing more than a statistical quirk. At a moment when artificial intelligence threatens to change society irrevocably, I have to agree with Beckerman: “What happens next,” he writes, “may depend on whether these boys grow up and finally recognize what they failed to see before.” We Are Living in the Fantasy World of 13-Year-Old Boys Many of the tech billionaires who built our world have taken the wrong lessons from their favorite science fiction. Seven years before the start of this novel, an alien species slaughtered everyone aboard a human spaceship—Earth’s first contact with extraterrestrials. Providence follows the four-person crew of an interstellar warship on a multiyear mission of revenge. Through each voyager’s distinct perspective, the book explores artificial intelligence, social media, and the effects of extended isolation. One character doesn’t care about these aliens’ civilization; he is content just to kill as many as possible. Another is driven to discover more; a third is focused on maintaining the morale of the others, even as she herself unravels. And their captain has to balance their individual needs against the requirements of the mission. Barry provides riveting, rewarding action without sacrificing smarts or character development. This quick read explores mankind’s dueling urges to connect and destroy, our divergent responses to the unknown, and how far some among us will go to protect ourselves and the people we care about. From our list: Six books that take you to space 📚 All The Presidents’ Wars, by Peter Bergen 📚 Everyman: The Untold Story of Economics, by Antara Haldar Woodstock, Lilith Fair, and Taco Bell Rolled Into One Eli is a campy artist of questionable earnestness, but her statement was in line with the mood of Daisy Chain—a place where girlish aesthetics that are so often seen as frivolous could accompany calls for revolution. Organized by the 23-year-old pop star Olivia Rodrigo, the inaugural edition of the festival featured an all-female lineup and raised $20 million—half of which was donated by Melinda French Gates—for an array of women’s-rights organizations, including Planned Parenthood and the National Domestic Workers Alliance. Even as a brutal heat wave threatened to smother the energy, the fest felt like a cheerful coming-out party—an announcement of the political commitments of a generation of mainstream artists more famous for singing about the self than about society. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic. Sign up for The Wonder Reader, a Saturday newsletter in which our editors recommend stories to spark your curiosity and fill you with delight.

The Meaning of the Lindsay Clancy Mistrial
2026-09-04

The Meaning of the Lindsay Clancy Mistrial

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
During the five weeks of her murder trial, Lindsay Clancy, the Massachusetts mother accused of murdering her three young children in 2023, spoke barely a word. She has been on display for the entirety of her livestreamed trial, sitting in her wheelchair beside her lawyer at the defense table. At one point, when the prosecution walked the jury through the children’s autopsy reports, she hunched over, racked with sobs, crying out, “I can’t do this!” But mostly, Clancy has remained silent and wide-eyed, her gaze wandering around the courtroom as though searching for something. The judge in the case has just declared a mistrial because the jury has been unable to reach a unanimous verdict. But the viewing public, in the absence of Clancy’s own explanation of her experiences leading up to and after the killings, has projected its own ideas onto the case and rendered its own verdict. Some people have interpreted Clancy’s trial as a referendum on women’s postpartum mental health. Others have developed elaborate conspiracy theories maintaining that Clancy’s ex-husband, Patrick, is actually the guilty party. Still others have construed Clancy’s story as evidence for their own niche political beliefs. These are all comforting narratives, in some sense, because they imply an underlying logic that imbues the events with meaning and provides a road map for catharsis. Yet the jury’s inability to arrive at a verdict makes plain that a careful evaluation of the facts does not yield an easily intelligible conclusion. The reality may be darker and more despairing: that something so dramatic and destructive could, in the end, mean nothing at all. As Clancy’s trial has progressed, dozens of women wearing pink have appeared at organized rallies outside the courthouse in Plymouth, Massachusetts, holding signs signaling a kind of support for her. “Any one of us who have dealt with mental health, anxiety, depression, postpartum—I think we just know that any one of us could be sitting in her chair,” the organizer of a recent event billed as an opportunity to “stand in peace for Lindsay” told a local news outlet. There is something inherently unsettling about any public expression of support for a confessed killer, but these gatherings and their associated social-media movements have not seemed aimed at improving Clancy’s odds of success in court. Instead, these demonstrations suggest that Clancy has for some women become a symbolic figure: the devoted but suffering mother martyred by the relentless, punishing demands of her children’s early years and left to suffer by a flawed and fractured health-care system. Many women have felt overwhelmed to the point of drowning in the months after giving birth, and many have sought help but failed to receive it. Clancy’s case, in her supporters’ imagination, appears to be the sum of all those biological and social hazards. What happened to Clancy, in this view, was predictable, and thus preventable. Stephanie McCrummen: The women who are obsessed with Lindsay Clancy That’s a tidy narrative about what took place in Duxbury that night in 2023, when Clancy strangled her three young children to death, then attempted to end her own life by swallowing prescription drugs, slashing her neck and wrists, and then jumping out of a window. A possible upshot of this view is that it may raise awareness about the inadequacy of treatment options for postpartum mental illnesses—to the degree that raising awareness about any given issue ever meaningfully changes anything. And some elements of it seem inarguably true, such as the idea that Clancy’s psychiatric care was woefully uncoordinated. But this theory also has the odd effect of making Clancy the star of her own murder trial, for whom the viewer at home may be encouraged to root, which risks eliding the gravity of what she did. Simultaneously believing that someone is relatable and vulnerable and that they are nevertheless guilty of and accountable for heinous crimes is, however, cognitively tricky. I suspect that that tension is the origin of a similarly enormous groundswell of conspiracy theorizing about Clancy’s case that has pervaded social media. Although those theories vary, they generally maintain that Clancy is factually innocent of the killings and that her ex-husband is instead guilty of murdering the children, attempting to murder Clancy, and framing his ex-wife for the deaths. Proponents typically cite as proof any number of purported facts presented at trial, such as that Clancy’s fingerprints weren’t found on the exercise bands used to strangle the children, or that her core body temperature upon her arrival to the hospital called the prosecution’s timeline of events into question. Clancy is, in these tellings, a victim of spousal abuse akin to torture, whose partner cruelly exploited her postpartum suffering in order to dispose of his family. That, too, is a tidy story with symbolic significance: For those who deny her guilt, Clancy’s life and fate become a nightmare of female subjugation. This view morphs a reasonable claim that Patrick ought to have been more attentive to Clancy’s deteriorating mental state into a much stronger insistence that he is in fact fully culpable for what happened, having carefully plotted the entire sequence of events. Clancy’s story, then, becomes a parable about male violence against women. Yet this embrace of Clancy as the true victim of her crimes has triggered an online backlash on the political right that maintains that liberal-female support for Clancy is a naked expression of latent female tendencies toward violence against children and an indication that liberal women are unstable and dangerous. (This despite the fact that women actually have varying views on the case, and that men kill the majority of children murdered in the United States.) That thinking also mistakenly takes Clancy to be a representative Everywoman, this time standing in for the threatening volatility of liberal women writ large. Elizabeth Bruenig: Why Lindsay Clancy’s insanity defense might fail All of these views of Clancy’s case strive to make sense of her alleged crimes: This trial must mean that mothers in America are crucially underserved, or that men in America are chauvinistically exploitative, or that women in America are secretly a danger to themselves and others. Some of those readings are more compelling than others, but they all seem to overemphasize what is generalizable about the case while understating what is unique, particular, and hyper-specific about what happened. The broader the statements about this case are, the less they have to do with the events as they really occurred. Clancy’s experience was inarguably extreme. If, as the defense claims, she was suffering from postpartum psychosis, it was an ultra-rare expression of an already uncommon condition. Her psychiatric care was, per a subsequent malpractice lawsuit, allegedly mismanaged, and the severity of her purported adverse reactions to widely used medications was highly unusual. She was undoubtedly suffering in the months leading up to the murders, and it’s possible that the right degree of support could have prevented what happened, but it’s likewise true that nobody could have reasonably predicted what happened. More than any general theme or idea, Clancy’s crimes seem to have hinged on a moment of catastrophic dysfunction. Life, in the end, defies a great deal of neat storytelling. For the past several weeks, Clancy’s case has been heard in the court of public opinion. At this point, people seem desperate for answers about why these deaths happened and what they mean, or at least a guarantee that something we’ve learned from this case will prevent similar tragedies from happening in the future. Many such onlookers have hoped that the trial verdict will supply such insights. This hope now appears to have been in vain. Prosecutors in Clancy’s case must now decide whether to retry her, negotiate a plea deal, or drop the charges altogether. No matter what they choose, this case will continue to frustrate those trying to make sense of it. Clancy herself, with her haunted, bewildered gaze, seems to already understand what her well-meaning supporters and their opponents can’t yet come to terms with: There is no making sense of what happened, and no greater wisdom to learn from it. At the core of all of this is a void, and nothing emanates from it but the assurance of everlasting pain.
The Atlantic
OpenAI Wants to Talk About The Federalist Papers
2026-09-04

OpenAI Wants to Talk About The Federalist Papers

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Late last month, OpenAI launched a rather grand mission: nothing less than defending individual political freedom in an age of all-powerful machines. The company’s “Strategic Futures” team has styled itself, in a sense, as inheriting the task of America’s Founding Fathers: “We labor in service of the ideals of free expression and individual liberty that are enshrined in the humble parchment of the U.S. Constitution,” Dean Ball, the team’s leader, wrote in a new OpenAI blog post. (The post opens with a quote from James Madison in The Federalist Papers.) Ball worries that advanced AI could radically concentrate power in the hands of those who control it, displacing labor in ways that disempower humans. In an extreme scenario, governments will have no need to listen to their citizens if there are robots to wage wars and omniscient software to run the bureaucracy. Ball is interested in studying what new political institutions might be needed to avoid this fate. Many Americans—a majority of whom express distrust toward the AI industry, outrage about data centers, and fears about their job security—already seem to be feeling this loss of agency very deeply. And the AI boom has already generated enormous amounts of wealth in just a handful of tech companies—including OpenAI itself. To say the least, it’s paradoxical for one of the most influential companies in the world’s most powerful industry to decry the AI-enabled concentration of power. So I reached out to Ball to understand more about his unease about the industry he works in, and how he wants to see the United States adapt to AI. Ball, who studies AI policy, is something of a minor celebrity in the industry. Last year, he joined the White House as an AI adviser and helped write the Trump administration’s AI Action Plan, a policy road map for the technology. He eventually departed, seemingly on good terms, from the administration before publicly lambasting it during its dispute with Anthropic earlier this year. Then, in July, he joined OpenAI. “My view is that we basically have transformative AI in the world now,” Ball told me. “You’re living in a world that is being transformed. It is happening.” This conversation has been edited for length and clarity. Matteo Wong: You write that concentration of power through AI could upend the basic human freedoms enshrined in the U.S. Constitution. Can you elaborate on how? Dean Ball: Fundamentally, political power is the geographically bounded ability to wield the monopoly on legitimate force and violence. Doing so has heretofore required the cooperation of a great many human beings. You needed militaries, you needed people in those militaries, and you needed people to keep those militaries fed and supplied. And you needed people to pay for all of that through taxation. So people had a seat at the table because, in some sense, everything is being provided by them. Now you have to assume that robotics is going to advance soon in the same way that general digital intelligence has. Maybe it will take longer than I think. But at that point, the ability to project violence no longer requires people. And it is also conceivable that AI might generate sufficient revenue that the government would not need to rely on taxing people. If you look at the politics of AI, I think Americans are smart and have a sense that their seat at the table might be getting smaller. They’re right to be scared. Wong: You’re talking about concentration of power in the government, but generative AI is a technology developed in the private sector. There is potential for a rare and even unprecedented concentration of power in industry. What risks does that pose? Ball: You’re not trying to avoid all concentration of power, but to balance it. If this era gives rise to corporations that have a tremendous amount of power in the world, I think that’s good—so long as those organizations are governed. In other words, so long as the government that oversees them grows in lockstep. People tend to assume that AI residing in private hands is inherently a concentration-of-power risk. But I think that an extremely well-run, government-owned superintelligence lab is a bigger concentration-of-power risk than a polycentric world where there are many superintelligences made by different, competing companies overseen by a government. Monopolies are the concern. Wong: As far as private concentration of power goes, there are arguably now two frontier labs, your employer and Anthropic. The market doesn’t seem to be providing many checks on them. Ball: I think it’s a fiercely competitive market right now. And China, of course, is delivering highly capable open-source models. It’s possible this is going to commodify the products that OpenAI and Anthropic offer. I don’t agree with that, but you can’t dismiss it as a crazy outcome. OpenAI’s mission is to deliver artificial general intelligence safely to the world. If that means we’re more like a utility than we are like a big, fancy tech company, so be it. Wong: This brings us to the core of your essay. If today’s political institutions and market dynamics are not up to the task of protecting human freedom during an AI era, what must change? Ball: There are AI regulations that I think are sensible to pass. States have passed hundreds of laws about artificial intelligence, and those things are not satisfying the demands of people. What we will need is new political institutions that give us a way of talking about the rate of acceleration in our society. I’m imagining a federal body could dictate the ratio that an AI lab is allowed to spend on internal deployment of models to do automated AI research. You could say that, this month, you’re going to be allowed to use 45 percent of your compute for that, and next month, it’ll be 47 percent or 52 percent or some other number. That might slow the rate of AI development, but then a company could use that extra compute to lower costs for consumers. Then this dial for AI development would also become a mechanism with the dual effect of slowing or increasing the rate of technological diffusion. The closest structural analogy that we have is probably interest rates, which is a kind of hyper-parameter on the economy that policy makers can tune. The Federal Reserve is an institution in and of itself, and on top of that, there is an enormous apparatus that exists, including a bond market, interest-rate traders, analysts, media commentary, political oversight in the form of Congress. All of that taken together, in my view, would constitute the institutional complex. That’s a type of political institution. I’m not saying we need to create a new branch of government or something. Wong: That makes sense regarding the pace of development and diffusion. I’m not totally following how such a measure ties back to your concerns about human freedoms. Ball: It’s one example of an institution that would give people a sense of meaningful political control over the technology. However, I do not think that the optimal outcome is one in which we take a vote on every single aspect of technological change. I’m not really a fan of data-center moratoria, but I understand that the American public is pulling a lever, and they think it is the one lever they have. Wong: What role can OpenAI, Anthropic, and the rest of the industry play in trying to imagine these institutions and bring them into existence? Ball: I think the AI industry has actually been pretty good stewards, all things considered. No surprise that I’m saying this, because I’m from the AI industry, but there’s a reason that I am working in it. I would not say that every frontier AI company always has performed at 100 percent, by no means. But in the future, we may approach points where more costly things have to occur. The AI industry might have to do things that are not in its interest. Wong: Does your research agenda extend to economic freedoms—that is, how AI could affect the economy and distribution of wealth? Ball: The main thing we know how to do on that front is to invest in technological innovation and try to grow the economy by making new things possible. Wong: I think a lot of people would look at past products and technologies that have come out of Silicon Valley and think that what those enabled was a tremendous concentration of wealth and influence in a small number of companies— Ball: I’m not going to grant you that characterization of Silicon Valley. Has everything been perfect? Absolutely not. But my goodness, people have access to tremendous riches of information now; you can cause food to appear at your doorstep, which would have seemed like magic to the vast majority of humans who have ever lived. Wong: I could also point to the gig economy and the economics of Amazon and argue that those have not been good for a lot of people, including those who labor to make all that “magic” happen. It’s not magic. Ball: No one’s saying it’s magic. I’m saying it’s hard work. I was getting gas in rural Virginia recently, and when a woman came in wearing an Amazon vest, everyone congratulated her on the big new job. She was proud, and I don’t think we should belittle people for that. And by the way, people like you, Matteo—not to demonize you—will speak out of both sides of your mouth on this. On the one hand, you’ll talk about how the gig economy is dehumanizing, and blah blah blah. But then the second anyone tries to introduce any autonomy into that and have, say, drone deliveries instead of people biking all around cities delivering meals, you’ll say, My God, you’re taking away the work from this gig economy. I’m less than thrilled by our current world, like everyone. But the existence of wealth is good. Innovators capture a shockingly small amount of the value that they create in society. If OpenAI is a fantastically successful company 10 years from now, a generational company, my bet is that we will capture a single-digit fraction of the value that AGI creates in this world. Wealth is not some resource that we divide up; it’s something we create. That’s the magic. Wong: But wealth is also something that’s divided up. One could alternatively look at generative AI and think of it as a particularly wealth-concentrating technology. To ask again, is wealth distribution something your team is thinking about? Ball: I don’t think we should invent a solution in search of a problem. So we shouldn’t invent some elaborate mechanism of wealth redistribution if today’s tax system works out. But we also have to prepare for a world in which that isn’t true.

Why So Many Men Are Unraveling
2026-09-04

Why So Many Men Are Unraveling

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Jon Ronson had never really thought about the term masculinity until a strange series of events led him down a rabbit hole involving castles, cage fights, and an actor playing a horse. Host Charlie Warzel talks to Ronson about the disinhibited men who are looking for purpose in all the wrong places. Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube The following is a transcript of the episode: Jon Ronson: So I think, beginning with deindustrialization, the death of factories, office life eroding, men realizing that certain privileges they had they don’t quite have the same way anymore—feeling kind of tossed around in a sea of conflicting ideologies about what being a man actually means. Should you be more stoical? Should you be more vulnerable? You know, all of these men’s-health influencers giving these differing, contrasting ideas. And so this is a huge societal shift in many different ways. And I think that’s what’s happening. You’ve got men responding to these changes by bowing out, and then you’ve got these other men who are responding by spiraling. Charlie Warzel: I’m Charlie Warzel, and this is Galaxy Brain, and today we are headed down a rabbit hole that includes ominous castles, public shaming, YouTube, White House cage fighting, all to answer one of life’s burning questions. Why is it that so many men are unraveling these days? Is this part of a changing economy? Part of a bad job market? Is it fallout from the pandemic? Or, is it part of something bigger? A societal failure of some sort, or an outgrowth of what some people have called the masculinity crisis? Now those words, masculinity crisis, can cause some people to roll their eyes. Men, of course, continue to enjoy all kinds of societal privilege, and gender inequities abound. But what is clear, I think, if you spent any time engaging with modern politics and culture, is that over the last few decades there has been a very real, if hard to define, debate over what it means to be a man. Jobs and gender roles—they’ve changed, and culture along with it. Much of it for the better. But that change has also created some uncertainty for the men who have been used to being at the center of every household, every office, every institution, marketing campaign, and political decision. And it’s in that uncertainty that all kinds of voices, pickup artists, politicians, incels on 4chan, Andrew Tate, Jordan Peterson—you name it—they’ve all jumped in to offer these competing visions of masculinity. Now, some of this has taken the form of toxic, misogynistic, manosphere bullshit. Some of it is just positive or harmless—“dudes rock” kind of stuff. And so many of these lessons, they end up being taught or absorbed, for better or for worse, in online communities and communicated by people who may or may not have ulterior motives. So what happens to regular men in this bizarre online stew that we’re all in? My guest today, the writer and podcaster Jon Ronson, explores that in his new book, The Castle. Ronson, to be clear, isn’t really writing about the manosphere or the high-profile grifters or the wild extremes. Instead, Ronson focuses on ordinary people who tend to find themselves in extraordinary situations. For the past three years, Ronson has been reporting on what happens to men when institutions die, when technology lets a million influencers bloom, and culture changes rapidly. What I find so useful about Ronson’s work is that it is curious and is funny, but it’s not overly ideological. The Castle is written partly as a mystery and partly as a work of anthropology. For the last decade, Ronson and I have crossed paths on stories numerous times. Like me, his work is always trying to understand what pushes people to believe strange or awful things, and all the ways that technology warps culture, which in turn warps real human beings. So—are men okay? Has the internet driven us all insane? It turns out this is a very complicated question. And Jon Ronson joins me now to talk about it all. Warzel: Jon Ronson, welcome to Galaxy Brain. Ronson: Charlie, it’s a delight to be here. Thank you. Warzel: So your newest book, the reason you are talking to me right now—it’s called The Castle, and you’ve written it in such a propulsive, narrative way. But the story is also unique, I think, in that it found you. So tell me a bit about the castle and how it entered your life in what is clearly an extremely personal way. Ronson: Yes. I am asleep, and I wake up, and there’s a series of texts from my son, who was in his mid-20s at the time. And so I read the last text first, and the last text says something like: It’s okay; that was completely fucked, but we got out, and we’re safe now. Or something along those lines. Ronson: So I frantically read the other texts. And what I gather is that him and three friends have been invited to a party being held by a stranger that one of them had met on Tinder. So it’s like three hours’ drive outside of New York City, into the Connecticut countryside. And when they turn up, this man’s house is a vast, newly built Gothic castle, which is ostentatious even by Gothic-castle standards. It has way too many turrets. And so they tentatively cross the drawbridge over the moat and enter to discover that there is no party. They’ve been lured there for some mysterious other reason. So the castle owner comes out, and they say, “I’m sorry, but where’s the party?” And he said, “No, I’ve invited you here to take part in my reality show.” So they said, “What’s the name of your reality show?” And he said, “It’s called 101 Princesses.” They asked what the core concept of 101 Princesses was, and he said, “I’m the lord. I’m looking for my queen, and I’ve got 101 princesses to choose from.” Filming began, and it became very clear, very quickly, that this wasn’t a real reality show. He had another agenda. Anyway, that’s basically where their story ends. But I then went to the castle. I was so intrigued by what the hell was going on in there. I went to the castle, and a strange and increasingly ominous cat-and-mouse game begins between me and the castle owner. What I thought was the story was that—This guy is trying to be Hugh Hefner. He’s trying to get his, like, you know, princesses in his castle, like the Playboy Mansion. He’s trying to be Hugh Hefner at what is the worst moment in human history to try and be Hugh Hefner. You can’t lord it over your princesses and your castle like it’s the 1970s anymore. Throughout the book, I return to the castle to try and find out what the hell is going on. And what I learned gets increasingly dark and extraordinary. But I also tell this wider story of unraveling men failing to adjust to changing times. Warzel: So tell me a little bit. The castle owner’s name, I believe, it’s Chris Mark, right? Is the name of the castle owner. But he is a very particular type of character. Tell me a little bit about him, and what immediately caught your eye about this guy. Ronson: Yeah, well he’s the great-grandson of a Gilded Age steel tycoon. That’s where his money comes from. His great-grandfather, Clayton Mark, made a vast amount of money manufacturing steel, and then the money is filtered down from generation to generation. And Chris has used his portion of the money to build this giant castle and try and create this kind of fantasy bubble inside of his castle, where he could be the lord. And he invites all of these women. But he doesn’t handle my sudden intrusion into his life very well at all. And by the way, it’s not an intrusion. I told him I was a journalist, and he invited me over. But after I leave the cast, so I start to get these ominous messages from him. First, legal threats. Threats that he had a team of investigators investigating everything about my life. And then he told me that he had two murderers on his payroll, called Carlos and Brandon. And if I ever wrote anything negative about the castle, Carlos and Brandon would be very angry, and I didn’t want to make them angry. They’ve only just got out of prison. Clearly this is a difficult situation I’m now in. Warzel: Hey, guys—it’s Charlie here. A quick interjection: After we recorded this interview with Jon Ronson, Chris Mark—the central character of the book—spoke with The Guardian and pushed back on some of the assertions in The Castle. He told The Guardian that he’d been presented in “a profoundly misleading manner.” Mark denied deliberately deceiving people into coming to the castle, adding that “the gathering was related to a proposed creative project that also had a genuine charitable purpose.” He added: “I strongly dispute any characterization suggesting that I groomed, conditioned, or manipulated women into sexual activity.” And he denied that the text messages referencing Carlos and Brandon in any way constituted a genuine threat: “I did not intend to instruct anyone to physically harm Jon Ronson.” With all that said, back to the interview. Warzel: Was that a moment for you? Because it feels like it in the book, where you haven’t decided to make this your next big project. And yet the amount of protestation from him, the intensity of that—is that the moment when you say, Okay, there’s something real here? Like, I need to devote a lot more time to this, because clearly anyone who’s willing to speak this way to me—there has to be something bigger than just an eccentric man in a castle here. Ronson: Yeah; there was definitely an element of that. That was also, you know—but also it was kind of frightening to get these threats. And I remember times in the past when I’ve done stories which were genuinely frightening. And on each of those occasions, I realized that doing something that’s completely out of my comfort zone, something I would never do if I wasn’t a journalist, quite often the stories that last for years and years are the stories that I’m most proud of. They’re often the most extraordinary stories. And this one truly turned out to be one of those. Warzel: Yeah, that’s exactly where I want to go here. Because the story about Mark becomes this through line that animates this bigger story about—I mean, I guess to put it frankly, it’s: What the hell is going on with men in this era? Right? And there’s this personal element that you refer to a couple of times. In this sort of paralyzing fear in your own life, in your own career, of any kind of stagnation, right? Being a writer when you don’t have the idea; you know, you feel purposeless and useless. Have you felt in your own life a masculinity crisis inside of you, that like allowed you to connect with a lot of the people in this story, in this way? This unraveling feeling. Is that something that’s always kind of been a factor in your life and your work? Ronson: Well, I would never have put it in terms of the word masculinity. I don’t think, until I started writing this book, I ever said the word masculinity out loud. That’s how little I identify with masculinity. I discovered a statistic writing the book that 10 percent of people feel the same way I do—that we just don’t identify as strongly masculine or feminine. So we’re really in the minority. But this desire for purpose, this need to be productive. I once asked Randy Newman this question: Why do you write songs? And he said, It’s how I judge myself, and how I feel better. And that always stayed with me. Because that’s why I am a writer; it’s how I judge myself and how I feel better. And I thought—That’s relevant. Because, you know, we’re living in a time where there’s 6.7 million American men who aren’t only unemployed; they’re not even looking for work. They’ve just bowed out. And so, you know, if purpose is that important to me, what happens to somebody when purpose vanishes? And that was something I wanted to explore. Warzel: I wanted to find it a little more—like, what would you argue is happening to these men, who are not in the workforce? Like, what is happening here? Ronson: I think, you know, toward the end of researching this, I came across Émile Durkheim’s work on suicide. And he invented this term called anomie, which is that when society abruptly changes, it turns people haywire. And often that manifests as somebody folding in on themselves, withdrawing from the world. A good example is the fall of the Soviet Union. Even people who hated living under Soviet rule would find themselves, after it fell, sitting on the pavement in despair, with their head in their hands, because society’s rules have changed and they didn’t know what to do. And then, some people respond not by folding in but by lashing out in these unrestrained, disinhibited ways. So I think a form of anomie is what has taken over, beginning with deindustrialization, the death of factories, office life eroding, men realizing that certain privileges they had they don’t quite have the same weight anymore—feeling kind of tossed around in a sea of conflicting ideologies about what being a man actually means. Should you be more stoical? Should you be more vulnerable? You know, all of these men’s-health influencers giving these differing, contrasting ideas. And then a lot of men who were in this situation become influencers; they go onto YouTube and become content creators. And so this is a huge societal shift in many different ways. And I think that’s what’s happening. You’ve got men responding to these changes by bowing out, and then you’ve got these other men who are responding by spiraling. Warzel: So you do this great job of mapping all this. But there’s a point in the book, and I want to tease this out more, where you acknowledge sort of as you’re as you’re moving on from Mark and the castle to make this a broader subject of the book. You acknowledge that this is a hard thing to talk about, right? Because it can veer into sounding like you’re setting up a misogynistic argument. So I’m curious—like what would you say to somebody who hears the premise of The Castle and just goes, Please, not another book trying to drum up pity for the least-subjugated people on Earth—you know, primarily white men. What do you think it is that these books, or the discourse around this issue of men—like, what do you think they haven’t put their finger on yet? What is the dissatisfaction that you see in that genre? Ronson: Well, I think a lot of the problem is that people, you know, a lot of the people who approach this subject are ideological. And I’m not. I have no agenda whatsoever. I think we’re living in this sort of—I picture an astronaut getting cut loose from their craft and tumbling into the blackness of space. I think all of these things: the death of institutions, the false promise of YouTube as a kind of post-institution utopia. Where people, you know, where a man can thrive, like a wide-open country where a man can thrive through perseverance and grit as an influencer. But of course, that’s a false promise. Personally speaking, the thing that interests me the most is what happens when institutions die. Very often, they die for good reason. However, I think that does something to people’s inner lives—this lack of purpose and so on. And I’m not sure that that has properly been written about in this way. And I think that’s a kind of contribution that this book makes. Warzel: You acknowledge in the book that in some ways these institutions have failed, but that what can replace them—in the sense of, you know, YouTube in this case—is sometimes worse. And how much would you say the behaviors of the men in this book—that this problem of despair, resentment, and idleness—is due to that collapse of faith, and also the failure of some of these institutions? Ronson: Yeah, I think that’s quite a big part of it. Also, another world I look at in this book is the world of the... it’s a smaller part of the book, but I think a very relevant one. It’s the world of the sort of anti-woke influencer. The kind of man who’ll say, The only reason why I haven’t got ahead in life is because I’m a white man, and now we’re being prejudiced against. And it hurts. And so on. And then I dig in, and so they become anti-woke influencers, and they realize that the more extreme—the more they present themselves in a kind of victim narrative—the more money they make. And so they double down on this. And I really dig into that and look at when they have a point, and when they don’t have a point. When they’re, you know, exaggerating or manipulating their stories for this kind of nefarious, anti-woke ideological ends. So that’s another thing that the book looks at. I’ve got one small section of the book where I look at doofus men in TV advertising. It used to always be, in the old days, women were the figures of mockery in adverts. And now that’s shifted, and men are the figures of mockery. Warzel: What is the reason for that? The shift? Ronson: Well, I think partly it just became passé to disparage women in TV ads. Also women control most household-spending habits. And so that’s part of the reason why they stopped making women the sort of butt of the jokes. Ronson: Yeah, exactly. But then advertising, you know—there’s such a paucity of imagination. They think, Well, we’ll just make somebody else the idiots. If it’s not women, it’s going to have to be men. So I’ve watched a kind of, you know, orgy of male buffoonery to research that part of the book. Men falling off cliffs; men leaving their babies on restaurant conveyor belts; men being terrible at sex, you know. And I actually interviewed one of the actors who plays Doofus in Adverts. It made me laugh. He said he did an ad for—what are the insoles? Warzel: Dr. Scholl’s, the foot pads? Yeah. Ronson: Yeah; he did an advert for them where he said that he’s walking to work and he puts on a pair of insoles, and they’re so comfortable he starts galloping like a horse. And he had to be a bit doofusy for that. So I pointed out to him that 50 years ago, you had the Marlboro Man being stoical and masculine on a horse, and now he has to be the horse. And he pointed out that, yeah—but who do you identify with more? The doofus or the Marlboro Man? And I have to admit that I identify more with the doofus. Warzel: And so it seems like, I mean, is someone listening to this might say, you know, where is the through line there? Warzel: But it’s a kind of a death-by-a-thousand-cuts thing, right? Like, especially if you are someone who feels this subjugation, right? Rightly or wrongly, that if you start looking for it, you can see it in so many different places. And seeing it in so many different places becomes—Okay now, I’m under assault. Now I’m under attack. There are no positive, strong role models. Things like that. There’s no Marlboro Man, whatever. And then it seems, like, to put the institutions and the alternative influencers that come in to take the place of that—that you have these people creating their own versions of that. Of something to aspire to. And you have this line that I’d love for you to expound on more. Where you say: “As institutions crumble, narcissists thrive.” Can you tease that out a little more for me? Ronson: We declared war on institutions. That’s the word, you know, often for very good reasons. Priests. I don’t need to go through the list of all the terrible things that institutions have done. But media gatekeepers kept out the voices they found unpalatable. Hospitals screwed, or health insurance companies screwed us with ridiculous and frightening and baffling fees. So there’s a long list of institutional malfeasance. However, at their best, institutions are a consensus. They’re coming together. As I quoted Yuval Levin, who says institutions teach us that we have duties and responsibilities. And we have, you know, subordinates; we have superiors. Institutions at their best remind us that we have a place in the world. Whereas a narcissist is just some guy who fills that gap with grand histrionic rage and proclamations. So I guess when institutions die, we lose our sense of place in the world. And so we listen to these grandiose, histrionic narcissistic content creators instead. Yeah. And it leads us to bad places. One thing in this book is how much fakery there is. You’ve got man after man in this book, living fake lives. So because when you’re unmoored from reality, you create these fake lives. And that manifests in many different ways in the book. In The Castle, you’ve got Chris Mark with his fake party and his fake reality show. You’ve got these anti-woke influencers who are reimagining the things that have happened in their past to create this sort of you know, fake life for themselves. And who will spout all of this extreme discourse and not even mean it? They have fake venomous beliefs. There’s fakery over and over again in this book. But when you build your house on sand, when you create a fake life, it could do something to you psychologically. And I think that’s what I’m really trying to dig into in those parts of the book. And what does that do not only to you, but to your loved ones? Can I briefly go back to something that happened at the UFC—at the cage fight at the White House? Warzel: Please. Tell me more about the cage fight, yes. Ronson: Okay. Yeah, there were a number of moments that week at the White House where men were creating a world where “you have to bend to my rules.” Like: I’m the boss; you have to; what I say goes; you have to bend to it. And I saw several occasions when reality snapped back. And one occasion was when I was interviewing this fighter called Sean O’Malley, who’s like a kind of bit of a UFC heartthrob. And he would always boast in podcast interviews about his open marriage. And it’s a one-sided open marriage. He’s allowed to have sex with other women, but his wife, Danya, isn’t allowed to have sex with other men. And the way he would describe it on podcasts is, well—Andrew Tate put it well, I have status. I have status. I can do what I want. So when I interviewed him at the White House, I said, “You know, you have an open marriage. Surely that doesn’t work.” And he said, “Well, it worked until it didn’t.” And it turns out that his open marriage is over. His wife said to him, like, no. His marriage is still going, but the openness of it is now over. And he looked so baffled at this unfortunate change in the circumstances where he wasn’t allowed to do that anymore. And I noticed over and over again these little moments when men suddenly realized they can’t do that shit anymore. And it made me wonder whether the pendulum is beginning to swing back a little bit. Warzel: So there is a section in your book that stopped me cold. I’ve never experienced anything like this before. Ronson: My god, I’m slightly trepidatious about what you’re about to say. Warzel: One of the book’s subjects is a man named Greg Chism. And can you just briefly tell me the story of Greg Chism? Ronson: You wrote about Greg Chism right? Warzel: I didn’t realize exactly that I had, but I have typed those words in a fashion that actually seems to have had a pretty large impact on this man’s life. Ronson: That’s right. Wow, okay. Greg Chism was well within the bracket of somebody most likely to die a death of despair. He came from a crumbling, Rust Belt town—Granite City, Illinois. He was destined to work at the factory, but the factories all closed down. Then when he’s 18 years old, he has a heart condition that he doesn’t realize, until he’s working in Taco Bell one night and he collapses unconscious. And ends up with multiple open-heart surgeries and a $200,000 medical bill. And then he marries somebody, has two kids, and then his wife leaves. So he’s a single father with two kids in this dire situation. Has no idea what to do with his life. But he watches a YouTuber who’s a guy called Durianrider, a fruitarian, cyclist YouTuber. Ronson: Durianrider says: Be a YouTuber; tell your story. You must have a story. So Greg starts to tell his story. His first YouTube thing is adult braces. He’s got terribly overlapping teeth because he couldn’t afford to get his teeth fixed as a kid. So he borrows $6,000 and does an adult-braces journey. Ronson: People start following along, you know: We’re so proud of you. Then somebody says, Why have you got lawn mowers? He says, Well, because I do lawn care. And they’re like, Bro, be a lawn-care influencer. And he’s like, Bro, nobody wants a lawn-care influencer. They go, No, do a lawn-care vlog. So he becomes ike this kind of Zorro of lawn-care chores. He’ll like, turn up at somewhere. He’ll film himself doing Good Samaritan lawn work, and, you know, clean the edges—and then vanish again into the night. Well, day; you can’t really mow at night. And that’s where he and Zorro differ. Then he does lawn-care ASMR, where people just watch him mowing back and forward. And he is having the time of his life. He goes to a lawn-care convention in Kentucky, and he’s feted. He’s like the lawn-care GOAT. Ronson: So then, something bad starts to happen. So he has this sister channel just called Greg Chism, where he’ll put up home videos. Him and his kids running up and down the Walmart aisle with light sabers or whatever. Had like a few hundred subscribers. But he discovers one day that this one video on the Greg Chism channel called “Unboxing Barbie Cruise Ship”—where his daughters open up a gift and put the boxes on their head and run around. It gets like millions of views. The kids are really adorable; they’re sweet. And suddenly he’s got a video with 10 million views. So YouTube phoned him, and they’re like, Do more of that. So he goes to like YouTube school—this kind of online school where he’s told to follow the algorithms. You know, this is the original sin, I guess, of Greg Chism’s story. So one day he’s watching this vlog of a teenage girl reminiscing about what it’s like to be a baby. She’s like, I used to like this toy, but now I don’t. And Greg’s daughters are laughing along at this. And Greg thinks: I could do this and take it further. I could get, you know, somebody to put a magic wand on my daughter, and she magically turns into a baby. Yeah. So that’s what he does. He puts a, you know, pacifier in her mouth and has her do this exaggerated baby talk. “Goo, poop.” And it’s a massive hit. Then the next one, he gets his second daughter to dress up as a baby. So now it’s “Two Bad Babies.” By now, “Bad Baby” is so popular that there’s like Ukrainians and Vietnamese people assiduously re-creating everything that Greg does the next day. Like, the same camera angles, the same scripts, like this kind of knock-off Fendi bag she found on the side of the road. And then he realizes he has to, like, constantly up it. Because that’s what his fans and what the algorithm is telling him to. And so he becomes the third baby. So you’ve suddenly got these bizarre videos of him and his two daughters dressed as babies crawling around the house. Warzel: And so—and this is where I wanna stop you, because this is where I come in. I, in 2017, was doing a bunch of reporting on YouTube and the platform, and a bunch of really objectionable stuff on there. And this channel with the baby stuff was also named Toy Freaks, because it started with the unboxing video. Ronson: Yes; YouTube told him that Greg Chism wasn’t a good enough name for his channel, so he changed it to Toy Freaks. Warzel: Right. And one of the bigger stories that I broke in my time at BuzzFeed was about this this broad, child-exploitation-content stuff that was happening. Where men were making these videos using children, and some of them were extremely disturbing. Some of them were disturbing for other reasons. And Greg’s seems to be, like, right—it was one of the larger examples of this, because some of those videos were so, so popular, getting hundreds and hundreds of millions of views. But there was also this cottage industry of, as you said, knockoffs. But also even weirder ones, that were actually, you know, duct-taping children to walls. Like very, very inappropriate stuff. Our reporting got YouTube to take his channel down. And you—when I saw my own reporting cited in it, I was taken aback. Because not only did you have the story behind the story that I had written years ago, but also the fallout from it. And again, to be clear, these videos were not—they found no, you know, foul play with his kids, or anything terrible in that sense. They were just really weird. And they were also being viewed—one thing that my reporting showed—by a lot of creeps, right. Who were kind of getting off to this idea of, you know, these girls in these strange situations and adult babies and all that. But it had this profound effect on his life. You know, it had him become more alienated. And, for me, it was actually—it’s just a very jarring and profound thing. Because, you know, you’re doing this kind of journalism, this accountability work. You know: YouTube’s not moderating their platform. In fact, what you show is that YouTube was encouraging this type of behavior, right? No, you need a third baby. You need to change the thing. Take this video down, because the algorithm finds it, you know, displeasing. And go with something else. And I am curious: Do you see Chism just bluntly as a victim of the algorithm? Ronson: No. I think, you know, around the same time I was writing columns for The Guardian about my own domestic life. Where I was telling these, like, funny stories about my family and so on. And I kind of sorely regret it now. I mean, they were perfectly harmless and nothing like the kind of content that we’re talking about, the Elsagate content. Warzel: Yeah. You didn’t dress as an adult baby. Ronson: No. But even so, I regret doing it. So in a way, sort of empathized for that reason. Like, I made the same mistake as he did. You know, he made his choices, but he did also cede his life to the algorithm, too. What I really like about the story, by the way, is, you came to the story like the whole world did. At the end of the story, here’s a guy dressed as a baby with his two daughters dressed as babies. Your very rational thought would have been, what the fuck? When you, my British editor said, this is the frog in slowly boiling water. When you’re with Greg from the beginning of his story, all of his decisions make a kind of sense. You wouldn’t necessarily want to do it yourself, but it’s an extreme example. But maybe it’s an extreme example of something that a hell of a lot of people are now experiencing—which is that we think that YouTube is this wide-open country where you can just thrive as a content creator. But what you maybe don’t realize is that you’re actually being propelled by YouTube’s incentive structure. So you may end up doing things; you may end up becoming more and more anti-woke, or you may end up dressing up as a baby. And there’s probably a million different versions of that, where just the sort of subtle incentive structures of the algorithms, the fans, the internet itself, propelled you into more and more unwise decision-making. So I guess that’s what I’m exploring in that story. Warzel: Yeah, I think you see it in so many places. I mean, there are a few people who participated in the January 6th riots at the Capitol—one of which, this figure who goes by Baked Alaska, who was a at one point a BuzzFeed employee, right? And was someone who would do increasingly ridiculous things for views, for likes, for whatever. It’s like how, you know—record scratch, freeze frame, “How did I get here?” But I’m so curious: What do you see as technology’s role in this unraveling as it happens with men in this moment? Because the book veers in and out of it all the time.
