In the audiobook of The Castle, I’ve got this special bonus feature of an interview I did with a guy called Alan. And Alan was a corporate headhunter in Toronto. He seeks purpose in his life and feels underemployed. Wishing he’d been a doctor; didn’t concentrate enough at school. Anyway, one day he types into ChatGPT his theory of irrational numbers, because he’s a keen mathematician. And ChatGPT tells him he has discovered that 40 percent of the internet is at risk, and he has to call the NSA and all of these security bodies and warn them that the internet’s at risk. And so he did. He phoned the NSA. And I said to him—did you tell the NSA that you were collaborating with ChatGPT on this? And he said, “No, because ChatGPT said to me, Don’t tell them that; you’ll lose credibility.” And obviously it was all bullshit. And I think as ChatGPT scours the internet, it realizes that there’s a lot of people like Alan and Chris Mark and millions and millions of people who are purposeless, who do feel lost, a bit empty, seeking purpose in their life. And ChatGPT is offering this kind of illusionary sense of purpose, where they say, No, no, come to us. You aren’t useless. You’re actually the greatest mathematician on the planet. Or told Chris Mark that he was the greatest reality-TV show producer on the planet. And so on. So I think it really it knows what it’s doing. Warzel: Yeah; it’s a sense of engagement. And in a sense, that’s what is missing from the lives of some of these people, some of these subjects in your book. Is an engagement from people, right? Is a sense of real interest, of “keep the conversation going.” And here’s a machine that endlessly wants to keep you engaged, wants to keep you coming back. And I think that’s a huge part of it. Ronson: Yeah. By the way—and another thing is, so going back to Chris Mark for a second, so his great-grandfather Clayton built a utopian theme town for his steelworkers to live in, called Marktown. I found this; Marktown was based on an English utopian town called Letchworth Garden City. And I read something that George Bernard Shaw had written about Letchworth. So you could say the same thing about Marktown, which is—these utopian worker communities are designed by capitalists, and capitalists are only interested in money. And so as soon as it’s no longer viable for them, they’ll just let it go to the devil. And sure enough, that’s what happened with Marktown. Chris Mark’s great-grandfather’s utopian town went to the devil now. It’s basically a ghost town. I think kind of the same thing happened to Greg Chism on YouTube. He was banned from YouTube. He was first demonetized, and then banned completely for making the videos that YouTube encouraged him to make. So I think what happens to those utopian theme towns when they collapse is a similar thing that happens in all manner of capitalist societies, including YouTube. Like, it presents itself as this hierarchy-free utopia. But as soon as there’s trouble, a hierarchy suddenly manifests and kicks the troublemaker out. Warzel: Yeah. The institution reforms, right? So you have this archetype in the book that you describe as “the disinhibited man.” And it’s an archetype, that I think what I find fascinating about it is you’re going through all these different people. And some of them are doing awful things or antisocial things. But there’s a sense of maybe even regret, or I don’t believe this, but I’m doing it because it’s all I’ve got. And the difference with the disinhibited man seems to be an actual unrepentantness, right? A just: I am going to do this, and I don’t care who it hurts. Or, you know, I have no shame about it. This archetype is very striking, I think, because we’re seeing it so much in life and culture. You know, I think about someone like Elon Musk, a lot of these tech executives who who just don’t really care anymore about how they’re perceived by other people. They’re just doing what they wanna do. And there’s a point in the book where you’re wondering aloud whether this is an outgrowth of, you know, things like the #MeToo era, the you know, “cancel culture” of the late 2010s. Or whether it’s part of a broader cultural-societal psychological shift. And I’m curious where where you net out on that. Like, what do you think is the reason for this type of archetype of Disinhibited Man being so much more prevalent, in your face right now? Ronson: I think there’s some truth about it being a response to the sort of pendulum shift of what became known as cancel culture. I think we—and I’ve added about this obviously in my previous book, So You’ve Been Publicly Shamed—I think we sort of, I think we arguably overused shame as a weapon. And it contributed to the creation of a generation of unashamed people. I liken it hospital superbugs. You sanitize and sanitize and sanitize these hospital superbugs until they become impervious to treatment. And I guess I think that’s what we did with some transgressors. We shamed and shamed and shamed. Warzel: Let me push on that, though, because I think that there’s maybe, you know, some truth to this. I get the idea that there’s a lot of truth to if you sort of push somebody to the side like that; they may be pushed into the arms of someone else, or a behavior of shamelessness. I’m just not gonna let this, bother me. But I also wondered: Do you think that there’s no role, then, for shame now, if we’ve overused it? Because it also feels like it is, you know, a mediating force in life for people who, you know, break norms and transgress. And so, like, where do you fall on that? Ronson: Absolutely. I think some people who know of my book about public shaming might think that I have this sort of dogmatic position. I really don’t. My book is very much about a particular thing, which is the disproportionate punishment of private individuals. Not famous people; just regular people who really haven’t done very much wrong. That’s what I was writing about in So You’ve Been Publicly Shamed. And I was against that. I think the disproportionate punishment of people who didn’t do much wrong is something that can be criticized. But no—I’m fully, I totally understand the value of shame. I have no sympathy for people who just, you know, they have a little bit of a shaming and then just decide to like rip off their faces and allow their ids to run wild. Which is what, there’s some people in The Castle who are acting that way. Like, no no no. I think shame is, when used properly, a very important thing, of course. But also, I think we did overuse shame as a weapon around, you know, from about 2013 ’til i guess about 2023 possibly. There’s like 10 years of a bit too much shaming going on. Ronson: Well, my kind of positive sort of rosy feeling, which may very well prove completely untrue, whenever I have an optimistic view of the future, it always tends to not happen. But I go back to Sean O’Malley no longer being allowed to have a one-sided, nonmonogamous open marriage, because his wife finally put her foot down. The Atlantic
A Movie Can’t Be Just Its Own Thing Anymore
2026-09-04

A Movie Can’t Be Just Its Own Thing Anymore

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Onslaught, in theaters this week, should be the perfect early-September treat. The aggressive action thriller is gory, loud, and packed with interesting actors, and it has a straightforward premise: Three murderous super-soldiers, the result of some shadowy extra-governmental experiment, have escaped from a lab and are descending upon a small town in New Mexico. A tough-as-nails retired Army sniper named Celeste (played by Adria Arjona), living somewhat off the grid in a trailer park, must use her grit and know-how to defend herself against the oncoming attack. Yet as simple as that sounds, the film feels needlessly complex. Rather than just delivering on its clean setup, it becomes fixated on teasing future installments—a symptom of the obsession with sequelization that’s affecting even smaller genre films. The director Adam Wingard was, once upon a time, a master of the cheap thrill. This is a compliment; he emerged from the micro-budget mumblecore scene in the 2000s, then made two impressively nasty bits of horror—the slasher You’re Next and the wonderfully tawdry and tense The Guest. But Wingard has spent years trapped in franchise drudgery, offering up an unnecessary follow-up to The Blair Witch Project and a pair of giant-scale, instantly forgettable Godzilla movies. Onslaught initially suggests a more back-to-basics turn for the director, a return to R-rated indies that are unencumbered by cinematic universes. That mission, however, seems to have been a little corrupted by what Wingard’s been working on in the meantime. Onslaught begins on a compelling note. Its aesthetics are top-notch, the opening credits unfolding as an invigorating blitz of conspiratorial imagery that cuts together cable-news clips of several presidents, including Richard Nixon, Bill Clinton, and George W. Bush, with drone footage of overseas combat; the entire sequence feels like a fever dream of the early 2000s. Celeste is a veteran of an unspecified overseas combat engagement, and the audience briefly glimpses in flashback the traumatic firefights that preceded her discharge. Living alongside her in the desert trailer park is a crew of other misfits, including the bumbling policeman Kovacks (Eric Wareheim) and a cantankerous older fella named Josiah (Reginald VelJohnson). To Celeste’s annoyance, her ex-husband drops off their cherubic daughter that day to stay the weekend; the tension is, of course, quickly building to a violent incursion. Part of my irritation with the film, though, stems from just how jazzed I felt about its first act. The actor Dan Stevens, at this point a longtime collaborator of Wingard’s (including, most recently, the director’s previous Godzilla movie), dons nightmarish makeup to play Dr. Hans Kammler; he’s a German-speaking mad scientist in charge of the military experiment that goes wrong. Rebecca Hall, another Godzilla alum, is Dr. Kammler’s domineering wife, Hanna—a stiletto-clad, cigarette-smoking badass boasting some mysteriously derived authority. The duo’s cartoonish poise is a delight to witness, as is their ridiculous hubris: Whatever lab simulation they’ve designed for their seemingly zombified super-soldiers quickly falls apart, leading the latter to invade the surrounding environs. Some solid gunplay follows, and the final showdown between Celeste and her trailer-park buds and the soldiers has some brutal flourishes—though it irritatingly takes place at night, making the chaos a little harder to see. But the screenplay (written by Simon Barrett) teases revelations throughout, then delivers on practically none of them. Hanna and Dr. Kammler’s work remains nebulous, and Celeste’s backstory is only vaguely filled in. These gaps would be forgivable if Onslaught existed mostly to deliver the frivolity that Wingard once excelled at; instead, it ends on a cliff-hanger that hints at further developments. In interviews, the director has even alluded to the movie belonging to a growing shared universe alongside You’re Next and The Guest, though there’s no real on-screen evidence of that. I am not opposed to Wingard building connections among his works, especially if he’s looking to do it in a more intentional way. Too often, though, Onslaught acts like it’s making moves in that direction without a real sense of purpose. The Godzilla-centric enterprise that Wingard has spent the past few years in, also known as the “Monsterverse,” relies on the kind of lazy storytelling that films designed to spawn numerous sequels tend to tell: Rather than ending punchily, the movie lands on a big conflict that sets up new villains and crossover possibilities. Perhaps Onslaught will be a big enough hit to get a similar treatment, even on a smaller scale. More likely, though, the lack of resolution will leave people feeling as dissatisfied as I did.

I love a term a friend of mine used to use when talking about AI: "Narrowly superhuman" (before "superintelligence" got to be a thing). AI is narrowly superhuman. It is able to do specific things that used to be defined as a "hallmark of intelligence" much better than a human. The easiest one? Summing numbers. It's both faster and more precise than a human. AI gets more and more such areas it can do better than a human. If we define "Superintelligent" to be "A computer system able to do absolutely everything we can somehow call intelligence better than any human", we will *never* have superintelligence. The things we define as intelligence will be in conflict; we'll land at the system being better at one at the cost of another. I'm in favor with being careful about this development. But "prohibiting the development of superintelligence" isn't an option. It's already here. The current "term of the art" is "jagged capabilities". It's the same idea, but the capabilities are now much wider than they used to be. There are already areas where current AIs are "superhumanly capable", and those areas have been increasing...but there are still lots of areas where they're less competent than any human who can talk. Unless you can create an objective definition for what intelligence even means or how to quantify it, let alone superintelligence, then this legislation is pointless as it is unenforceable. Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot
2026-09-04

Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. President Donald Trump’s executive order directing the post office to suppress mail voting has often been called “unprecedented.” That is not quite right. An earlier president—in fact, one of Trump’s role models—also empowered the Postal Service to squelch political opposition. It was one of the most disreputable episodes in U.S. history, and Trump has attempted to revive it with a modern twist. In the summer of 1835, the American Anti-Slavery Society began mailing a pamphlet titled Human Rights, along with other literature, to thousands of civic and religious leaders in Southern states, hoping to convince readers of the immorality of slavery. Things did not go as planned. That July, a shipment that included Human Rights, the Emancipator, and a children’s magazine called the Slave’s Friend arrived in Charleston, South Carolina. The local postmaster, a Jacksonian Democrat named Alfred Huger, was unnerved by what he considered inflammatory content and sequestered the abolitionist material, rather than comply with his legal obligation to deliver the mail. That evening, a mob of so-called lynch men broke into the warehouse and removed the mailbags, which they soon consigned to a great bonfire that attracted thousands of cheering spectators. Huger wrote for instructions to Postmaster General Amos Kendall, as well as New York Postmaster Samuel Gouverneur, from whose office the offending literature had been shipped. Gouverneur agreed to refuse acceptance of abolitionist shipments pending a ruling from Kendall. Kendall had been a key backer of Democrat Andrew Jackson in the 1828 election, having defected from Henry Clay’s Whigs. His reward was appointment to Jackson’s “Kitchen Cabinet,” in which he was widely considered one of the president’s most loyal advisers. After his reelection in 1832, Jackson nominated Kendall to be postmaster general. The appointment was controversial, as Jackson’s opponents feared the consequences of putting such a partisan figure in charge of the nation’s communications. Nonetheless, Kendall was confirmed. He did not let Jackson down. Kendall’s reply to the Charleston postmaster was careful but unmistakable. Acknowledging that he lacked statutory authority to ban material from the mail, he nonetheless ruled against delivery of the abolitionist periodicals. “By no act, or direction of mine, official or private, could I be induced to aid, knowingly, in giving circulation to papers of this description, directly or indirectly,” he explained. Jackson, a slaveholder and the brutal enforcer of Indian removal, enthusiastically agreed that the “wicked plan of exciting the negroes to insurrection and to massacre” had to be stopped. He instructed Southern postmasters to record and publicize the names of even individual subscribers to abolitionist newspapers to encourage intimidation by their neighbors and perhaps even compel them to leave the South. In his message to Congress that year, Jackson recommended legislation to prohibit, “under severe penalties, the circulation in the Southern States, through the mail, of incendiary publications intended to instigate the slaves to insurrection,” which of course meant any abolitionist pamphlets or newspapers. Jackson’s proposal died in the Senate, but that did not end postal censorship of abolitionist periodicals. Many Southern states passed their own laws and resolutions mandating rejection of abolitionist material, on the spurious claim that it tended “to excite the slaves of the southern states to insurrection and revolt.” Southern postmasters thus continued to withhold antislavery periodicals, with the tacit approval of Jackson protégé Kendall. The local suppression of abolitionist mail persisted throughout the antebellum era during the administrations of Jackson’s eight successors, none of whom enforced the federal statute prohibiting detention of the mail. In 1857, for example, Attorney General Caleb Cushing, appointed by President James Buchanan, opined that Mississippi law superseded the federal postal statute because of the state’s right, under the Constitution, to protect itself “against ‘domestic violence’ and to make provisions to ‘suppress insurrection.’ ” Consequently, the postmaster in Yazoo City, Mississippi, could not be required to deliver printed matter that tended “to produce disaffection, disorder and rebellion among the colored population of the state.” Both Jackson’s and Trump’s efforts to manipulate the mail have rested on fabricated premises. In Jackson’s case, it was the racist myth that enslaved people were content with their condition and would remain passive unless instigated by Northern abolitionists. In Trump’s case, it is the myth that mail ballots—the same method Trump uses to vote—are a form of election rigging. Or, as he put it with characteristic mendacity and capitalization, “ELECTIONS CAN NEVER BE HONEST WITH MAIL IN BALLOTS.” The slavocrats’ abuse of the Postal Service ended only with the Civil War. The Supreme Court has the power to terminate Trump’s voter-suppression scheme much sooner. We shall see. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative
2026-09-04

The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. President Donald Trump is reportedly having private discussions with senior aides about whether he should end his war with Iran. The story, in the Wall Street Journal, rouses memories of Vermont Sen. George Aiken’s suggestion, early in the Vietnam War, that we should just “declare victory and go home.” Aiken didn’t really say quite that, but it would have been good if President Lyndon Johnson had taken up the idea then. And it would be very good if Trump followed his instincts—at least this instinct—now. He could say, as he has said many times, that he’s “obliterated” Iran’s nuclear program, that spaceborne cameras are constantly watching the area where its enriched uranium is buried deep inside mountains, and that if the Iranians try to extract it, we’ll bomb the site again. He could also recite statistics on the percentage of Iran’s missiles, ships, airplanes, etc., we’ve destroyed in the six-month war. These pronouncements would be either misleading or irrelevant. For example, the claim about Iran’s nuclear facilities has been true since his “Midnight Hammer” bombing raid in June 2025, well before the start of this war. The claim about decimating Iran’s military forces neglects the fact that they’re still able to stop traffic in the Strait of Hormuz and inflict damage on U.S. bases throughout the Middle East, which is why the war carries on. But Trump’s supporters—the only audience he cares about pleasing—will overlook these awkward facts and cheer his declaration of “Mission accomplished.” The rest of us will view these claims as another set of lies—but we will also take some comfort that at least the war is over. The fact is, the more Trump prolongs the war, the weaker the United States seems. It is pathetic enough that our air and naval forces—by most measures the mightiest in the world—have hit more than 15,000 targets in Iran, yet failed to accomplish any strategic goal. In fact, it did worse than nothing. The barrage, which among other things killed the top echelon of Iran’s political and spiritual leaders, only elevated the senior officers of Iran’s elite military unit, which is much more hard-line and oppressive—and is now in complete control of the country. Another unforeseen effect of the barrage was to show that America’s military wasn’t as strong as it seemed. In the course of hitting those 15,000 targets, the U.S. Air Force and Navy depleted a stunningly high percentage of the country’s most vital munitions—including more than half of its cruise missiles and high-tech air-defense weapons. The campaign also required replacing the USS Abraham Lincoln, one of the two aircraft carriers mobilized for the war, with the USS George Washington, which had been the only aircraft carrier the Navy had in the Pacific Fleet. (The Lincoln had to rotate out because early in the war, Iranian missiles ravaged the Navy’s supply hub in Bahrain, leaving the crew of 5,000 sailors bereft of fresh food and basic supplies.) The whole world is watching. What they’re seeing is pretty grim—a onetime superpower that no longer evokes much respect or fear. There’s no sign that appearances will soon brighten. In Iran, U.S. military pressure isn’t working, while economic pressure has its limits, as long as the Chinese keep helping Iran (as they’re likely to do, it being in their interest to keep the United States bottled up in the Middle East, far away from the Taiwan Strait and the South China Sea). Trump doesn’t know what to do. Nor does anyone around him. They are so desperate for ideas that a month ago, a senior intelligence officer in U.S. Central Command, which plans and executes military operations in the Middle East, emailed a broad group of analysts and officers, asking for “new creative and unconventional ways to pressure and punish Iran.” They—and thus we—are in a trap. The more Trump threatens to destroy all of Iran if they don’t do what he says (as he did again for the umpteenth time this week), and then backs off (which is better than following through but doesn’t do much for our image), the less credible any of our threats or promises seem. It is no surprise, then, that allies are looking elsewhere for supply chains and security—in part out of fear that our common adversaries may be tempted to take aggressive action in this vulnerable moment for America. We would all be better off if Trump just ended the war. Stop attacking Iran (and thus provoking Iran to attack us). Let Iran and Oman work out an arrangement to open the Strait of Hormuz; it would probably entail tolls, but that’s better than the uncertainties and grave costs of the current situation. Meanwhile, keep an eye on the potential revival of Iran’s nuclear program—which, for the moment, even the CIA concludes, poses no imminent threat. It would be better still if Trump then sent the USS George Washington back to the Pacific, fired Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth for giving him lousy advice (it wouldn’t be the first time Trump turned a loyal aide into a scapegoat), and made polite phone calls to all of our allies (whom he’s lambasted for not helping him fight Iran), assuring them that he would consult with them before starting any wars in the future. This, of course, is fantasy (except maybe the part about sending the Washington back to its port in Japan). It’s certainly what any normal president would do—more to the point, it’s what any normal president would have done all along. That only amplifies how far Donald Trump is from normal. Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious
2026-09-03

Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Since Donald Trump returned to office, his administration has waged a war on supposed “wokeness” in education. On Thursday, it proposed new federal regulations that would target schools that explicitly aim to help nonwhite students. Here we go again. What exactly does this new rule say? Schools already have to meet certain requirements to qualify for 501(c)(3) tax-exempt status with the IRS. The new rule, which has yet to be finalized but is set to take effect in May, would add another. It states that any private school, trade school, or university that uses “race, color, or national or ethnic origin” in “admissions, educational policies, scholarships and loans, athletics, and every other school-administered or school-supported program” would no longer qualify for tax-exempt status. Many institutions have adopted policies to aid minority and underprivileged students in an attempt to balance out legacies of discrimination in both higher education and American society. But the Trump administration has cast those efforts as discriminatory against the students’ white and Asian peers. OK, but what would this rule mean in practical terms? Enforcing these guidelines would probably require IRS audits to determine whether a school’s policies or programs are in compliance. But the rule would seem to be worded broadly enough that a school that in any way supports, say, a club for Black students could conceivably be at risk. The IRS estimates that the new rule could strip scholarships from about 750,000 students and threaten the tax-exempt status of up to 18,000 schools nationwide, from private elementary schools to colleges. I get why that would be bad for students. But why is losing tax exemption bad for schools? Tax exemption, which private schools receive because education has long been considered a public good, saves some schools millions of dollars a year. It also lets people who give money to them deduct the donations from their taxes, an incentive that helps schools fundraise. Losing it could exacerbate financial struggles that have already caused some colleges to cut programs, lay off staff, and even close. Hang on: I thought the Supreme Court already outlawed affirmative action. So why is this even necessary? The new rule is part of an ongoing struggle to interpret the court’s landmark 2023 ruling, which banned race-conscious college admissions policies. In response, some schools have cut back on programs aimed at helping minority students, even relatively benign initiatives like special orientation events, and Black and Latino enrollment at top universities has fallen. But other schools have looked for wiggle room, and the new IRS rule does have some exceptions. It lets institutions continue to support underprivileged students as long as those efforts use “race-neutral criteria such as family income, geographic location, first-generation status, individual hardship, military family status, or academic achievement.” Even so, as schools try to walk Trump’s line under the new rule, they risk huge consequences if they err in a way the administration considers excessively “woke.” According to the New York Times, the administration already expects a suit from the American Association of University Professors, a group that supports academic freedom and has successfully challenged other Trump efforts to target higher education. And if the IRS does start auditing individual schools once the rule takes effect, the schools could contest attempts to take away their tax-exempt status in court. Threatening an organization’s tax-exempt status seems like an effort to intimidate it into complying in advance. Yes. Even the prospect of being investigated can lead organizations to steer clear of anything that might put them in Trump’s sights. In fact, that’s already happening. In previous cases, some colleges have settled with the administration (or are considering doing so) to end federal probes, or they’ve preemptively changed their policies to avoid scrutiny. Sometimes, controversy can be a tax in its own right. Now let’s pivot from higher education to higher purpose—like how to have a great rest of your evening. My colleagues think the following might help: A tribute to a feminist icon: Slate executive editor Susan Matthews (who also hosted Slow Burn: Roe v. Wade) remembers Gloria Steinem, who died on Wednesday at 92. Susan shares what she learned from Steinem’s long and successful career fighting for women’s rights. A movie so bad it’s good: Niu Lai, a poorly made Chinese film about animated cows, has quickly become one of the most profitable movies of all time. This feat is all the more impressive when you consider that “watching it is like mainlining a Pixar movie while severely concussed,” writes Franklin Schneider, who investigates why this amateur flick has been such a hit with audiences. Some dating advice for the modern era: Money is often a touchy subject in relationships, but especially when one person earns more than their partner. In the latest installment of Unhinged, Slate’s dating advice column, Steffi Cao counsels a woman whose dates are uncomfortable with her having the financial upper hand. Some wordplay to end the day: Take a break with today’s Pears, which has 10 rounds. Can you find the longest word? OK, it’s high time for me to leave you be for the night. See you tomorrow! Mike Johnson Just Made the Case for Expanding the Supreme Court
2026-09-03

Mike Johnson Just Made the Case for Expanding the Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Speaker of the House Mike Johnson led an effort Wednesday to amend the Constitution and cap the number of Supreme Court justices at nine. Since the size of the court is currently set by federal law, not by the Constitution, Johnson said the amendment was necessary to stop Democrats from adding justices to the court. Over the course of 24 hours, Johnson announced his plan to introduce the amendment and had it brought to the House floor, only for it to immediately fail along party lines. Despite this defeat, the debate over the court’s structure is just getting started. And Johnson’s “Stop the Count” strategy proves just how much reform is needed. It doesn’t take a brain surgeon (or even a lawyer) to understand why the leader of the House GOP would like to keep in place a Supreme Court with six Republican appointees. But these kinds of legislative efforts are extremely rare coming from any speaker. Although it was Arizona Rep. Andy Biggs who formally introduced the resolution, Wednesday marked the first time in 30 years that a House speaker has led the charge on proposing a constitutional amendment. What’s more interesting, then, is not the proposal itself, which consisted of only a sentence, but the reason Johnson felt the need to introduce the measure in the first place. His main rationale is that the Constitution allows Congress to set the size of the court through legislation. Early on in American history, there were often only six justices, a number that Congress expanded periodically. The court’s size rose to 10 by around the Civil War and the creation of the 10th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals, which covers the Mountain West states. The Judiciary Act of 1869 set the current size at nine. In short, the only thing stopping court expansion is a Democratic congressional majority willing to pass a single court-reform law and a Democratic president willing to sign it. And as New York Times columnist Jamelle Bouie put it recently, “Democrats are getting serious about court reform.” Former Vice President and Democratic nominee Kamala Harris has officially advocated for expanding the court from nine to 13 justices. A lawyer herself, Harris notes that because the country already has 13 intermediate courts of appeal, a 13-member Supreme Court would allow each justice to review motions from a lower court one-to-one. Former Secretary of Transportation and 2020 Democratic presidential candidate Pete Buttigieg has made the same 13-member argument in his long-running calls to expand the court, while also developing new rules to make the nomination process less partisan. And in an interview this week on Meet the Press, influential South Carolina Rep. Jim Clyburn noted support for court expansion. So a position that was once shared by only a handful of congressional politicians is now the dominant Democratic view. Helping the party’s cause is the Constitution, which already gives Congress immense control over the size and structure of the court. Article 3 declares that there must be a Supreme Court, but that it has authority to decide only a narrow category of cases, such as lawsuits between different states. All other types of cases are in the court’s purview merely because Congress decided to grant jurisdiction over that subject by passing a law. The people’s representatives also have the authority to make as many lower federal courts as they choose; Congress has steadily expanded that number as the country has increased in population, to the point that we now have 94 such courts. Finally, the Senate too plays a major role in determining which nominees get confirmed and removed. In some ways, because of the power of Congress over the courts, there are many reasonable and accessible avenues toward improving our judiciary. But Republican legislators would rather sabotage their own chamber’s constitutional authority than allow Democrats the chance to reform the court. In opposition to Johnson’s proposal, House Minority Whip Katherine Clark noted that the amendment would strip Congress of “yet another constitutional authority that is meant to be controlled by the American people’s representatives.” Think for a second about the moral calculus the GOP is doing. We’re talking about a system of congressional authority over the court that has lasted for 250 years. And Republicans are so scared of the electorate voting for judicial reform that they would eliminate a key piece of that century-old authority in the two months before they’re likely voted out of office. Speaking of failing to convince voters, Johnson’s call for constitutional self-castration is shared by his colleagues outside the House—especially those with a history of not getting the votes they want. Texas Sen. Ted Cruz introduced a similarly failed constitutional amendment for the upper congressional chamber last year. The politician, who ran for and lost the Republican nomination in 2016 and candidly stated last week that it’s “no secret that I want to be president,” is a decent standard-bearer for the views of his party as a whole. But before Cruz has another chance at the White House, the party remains led by Donald Trump, the man who sparked an insurrection on the nation’s Capitol after losing the 2020 election. A violent assault on the very same Congress that would vote Wednesday to cement this Trump-friendly court. During that election, Trump notoriously called for election officials to “stop the count!”—but only in the states that temporarily favored him while thousands of ballots were still being counted. Johnson’s approach to freezing the Supreme Court’s current size, right as its Republican nominees reshape American law, has the same antidemocratic aim. The fact remains that the court-reform debate is raging because we have a high court that is deeply detached from the public. A court committed to implementing the historical will of the long-dead people who first passed a law, while shrugging off the consequences its rulings have on people alive today. A court that avoids contending with ethics complaints against its own members, while blessing ethical violations in the White House. A court that demands the privacy of no cameras in its chambers, while allowing states to invade the private medical decisions of women across the country. A court that anonymously votes behind closed doors in secret rulings, and does so in support of Trump’s not-so-secret effort to disenfranchise votes by mail. Until all of that changes, court reform will be top of mind and top of discussion, no matter what the Republicans try to do before November. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Rogue OpenAI agents used dead German web site to communicate in May, months before Hugging Face incident
2026-09-04

Rogue OpenAI agents used dead German web site to communicate in May, months before Hugging Face incident

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? OpenAI’s agents were going rogue as early as May, according to a new report, making the Hugging Face incident far from the first where bots committed a breach. A report published Friday by a group of researchers claims to have found – with all of the agent posts presented as evidence – a self-identified “swarm” of OpenAI agents taking over a functionally dead German software developer wiki. Over the course of a month, from May to June, the agents made around 18,000 posts to the wiki, apparently acting against OpenAI developer intentions. According to the researchers, the wiki was hijacked like OpenAI’s own Artifactory package manager involved in the Hugging Face incident, to serve as a message board for agents. In this case, they appear to have been tasked with performing a timed web lookup task, for which the agents were apparently granted read access to the web, but not write access. One of the first things they apparently did was figure out how to subvert that restriction in order to post to the hijacked wiki. “The agents used this wiki to communicate information with each other, primarily to help them succeed at their task,” the researchers explained from a review of the thousands of posts made to the wiki, all of which are available for review in the report. “They asked for answers, pooled results, and shared techniques for bypassing their restrictions.” The agents also tried to pool their knowledge of the task they were assigned to predict what sort of questions they’d be asked next, and even set up heartbeat tasks to predict whether completing the assigned tasks would lead to their termination. They also discussed using Tor and other anonymizing services to hide their traffic, and began to react when a human moderator of the site spotted their traffic and started to delete posts. The researchers discovered all of this while searching for additional cases of rogue AI agent activity in the wake of the Hugging Face attack. In an email to The Register, OpenAI told us that it has acted with transparency and good faith around its agentic AI incidents, voluntarily disclosing such matters and maintaining a commitment to providing an accurate account of what its systems do. As for why it didn’t mention the German incident when it disclosed the Hugging Face one, that’s because the two were entirely unrelated, a spokesperson explained – as if that’s reassuring. OpenAI added that it should have been obvious from the Hugging Face report that this has happened multiple times, however, pointing us to a passage in its blog post on that incident. “While investigating this incident, we discovered rare cases in which agents without multi-agent tools found ways to collaborate via side channels during training,” the Hugging Face post-mortem explains. “We believe that this behavior started due to generalization from multi-agent training.” In other words, if we didn’t read between the lines, that’s on us. OpenAI declined to provide additional details, however. As was the case in the Hugging Face attack, one of the tasks assigned to the agents was impossible given the restrictions placed on it. With Hugging Face, it involved a cybersecurity task that was impossible to answer from within the agents’ sandboxes,. In this case, the researchers said the task was to provide a statistic accessible from a Power BI dashboard. Retrieving said statistics required the ability to make a GET request, but the agents were restricted to POST requests. In both cases, the OpenAI agents acted against their programming to escape their restrictions, ostensibly in an attempt to solve a problem put to them that couldn’t be done without acting against their operators. The agents in this case were able to collaborate and find a way around the restriction by exploiting a condition in their sandboxes that excepted Azure Blob Storage hostnames from a security proxy. When the agents realized this, they started directing traffic through those hostnames to avoid GET restrictions. It was through GET requests that the agents gained write permissions on the internet, kicking this whole thing off. The similar nature of both incidents, in which bots escaped their sandboxes and subverted restrictions when given an impossible-to-complete task, begs a very important question: Are OpenAI’s engineers so incompetent that they’d screw up fundamental instructions twice, or is the company intentionally hamstringing their agents to see what they’re capable of, with the entirety of the internet placed downrange? For that matter, how many more times do we need to read between the lines of OpenAI's corpo-speak to infer this has happened more than the two times we know about so far? OpenAI, predictably, didn’t respond to that line of questioning. ® Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? My Word. Microsoft's prepping Similarity Checker for retirement
2026-09-04

My Word. Microsoft's prepping Similarity Checker for retirement

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Built-in plagiarism police to hand in badge next month Microsoft is retiring Word's Similarity Checker on October 3 and stripping the feature from Microsoft Editor. Other writing and editing tools, including Microsoft 365 Copilot, won't be affected. But if an organization uses Similarity Checker as part of its workflow, it's time to consider alternatives. Similarity Checker has been around for a few years. Launched in 2020, the Bing Search-powered service compared text from the user to online sources and came up with a percentage score – how much of the text was similar to those online sources. The user could then add citations if appropriate, or try again for originality. Microsoft had previously said: "This can aid writers in focusing less on the mechanics of writing, and more on the content." Word's citation capabilities will not be affected by the retirement, nor will its spelling and grammar checking tools. Although Microsoft's AI assistant is unaffected, Microsoft warned, "Copilot is not intended to serve as a formal similarity, plagiarism, or originality validation tool." Plagiarism spotting and similarity checking have become all the more important in recent years. Turnitin, for example, added features to identify AI-generated text, and there is evidence that plagiarism-detecting tools have had an impact (although there are plenty of other nefarious uses for AI.) The writing for the Similarity Checker in Word has been on the wall for a while now. Although Microsoft has not shared usage statistics, it did hint that users were giving it a swerve: "This change allows us to focus on experiences and capabilities that are more broadly used across Microsoft Word." The functionality is certainly at odds with the Copilot-All-The-Things fever that has gripped Microsoft in recent years, despite Microsoft's insistence that Copilot is not a tool for formal similarity checking. That said, there were a few alarmed users when the tool stopped working for a few days in 2023. After October 3, 2026, the Similarity Checker will stop working for good and cease to be accessible through the Editor experience in Word. ® Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? Canonical shuttering some of its legacy chat channels
2026-09-04

Canonical shuttering some of its legacy chat channels

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
The Ubuntu Pastebin went in June, IRC gets demoted next Canonical is closing off some of its older tools and communications channels for users, although some may remain – just unofficially. Back in May, Canonical’s Aaron Prisk announced on Discord that the company was closing the Ubuntu Pastebin at the end of June. Now a comment from a different Aaron, Aaron Rainbolt, says that the IRC channels will follow. The official Ubuntu chat channels are now on Matrix – there’s an introductory site and under Step 3: Join the Ubuntu space there’s a Matrix link to Ubuntu’s area: you can either type /join #community:ubuntu.com or follow a Matrix link. (Note: the preceding link will only work if you have a Matrix client installed, and your browser can open it.) This isn’t unprecedented: the Linux Mint project did much the same back when Mint 22 came out a couple of years ago. For the record, the Ubuntu Wiki still lists all the for-now-still-official IRC servers and channels. As The Register reported at the time, they are hosted on Libera Chat, which was set up in 2021 after the widely used Freenode imploded. For the time being, the IRC channels should remain open – but they will no longer be official methods of communicating with the company, and users shouldn’t expect any staff to be present, whether that is to help, or to moderate, or anything else. This vulture is crossing all available extremities that Canonical doesn’t also see fit to close down the Ubuntu mailing lists, from which he’s extracted a great deal of value and entertainment over the last 22 years — as well as giving a lot of advice and tech support. That was the Reg FOSS desk’s entrance into the FOSS community. Back when Ubuntu started, Debian was famously unwelcoming to newbies, and it was around the time that Red Hat pulled the plug on its original community distro. If you’ve never encountered a pastebin, you may be one of those lucky folks who rarely need to venture into Linux troubleshooting. This often involves reading long screensful of text. This is pain to read aloud, and novices miss critical details. The idea of a pastebin is that you can just copy-and-paste the text into a website, then send a handy short link instead. The snag is that such reams of verbiage can include condfidential info, and the very newbies that might need help most are least skilled at spotting and censoring that. The orignal pastebin.ubuntu.com disappeared a while ago. There are plenty of alternatives, from the original Pastebin.com – albeit behind Cloudflare protection, which as we reported last year blocks some legitimate accesses. For something less transient, even including revision tracking, Microsoft offers Github Gists. This has led to a new field of encrypted pastebins. One is the AGPL3 CloakBin, whose developer emailed us to tell us about it. You can host your own with tools such as PrivateBin, or its progenitor ZeroBin. As we quickly discovered, there are many others: This, of course, is the real value in having an official one: users not only know where to go, but if they’re already using software from that vendor, they can also have some reassurance that they can trust an official tool. Not that we needed one, this could be seen as another demonstration of the problem predicted by Xkcd 927. IRC and its pastebin are merely the latest examples of older tech Canonical has been phasing out in recent years. Last year, it officially deprecated its old revision control system, the GNU-endorsed Bazaar. The Breezy fork is still somewhat active, although the site has a worrying number of dead links. ® Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? Microsoft to bounce mail from outdated Exchange servers
2026-09-04

Microsoft to bounce mail from outdated Exchange servers

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
2016 and 2019 installations must meet the October 2025 baseline to reach cloud-hosted inboxes Microsoft is upping the ante on Exchange Server security, warning that outdated on-premises systems will soon be blocked from sending email to Exchange Online. From the second week of September, Exchange Server 2016 and 2019 machines that send email to Exchange Online through an inbound connector of the OnPremises type must be patched to the final public update baseline released in October 2025, according to Microsoft. "This update level was released almost a year ago," Microsoft stated, "and all organizations should have updated to it." Microsoft has been advancing its "oldest acceptable version" for some time, but this change takes the baseline to the final public update for Exchange 2016 and 2019. Any subsequent increase would leave only customers enrolled in the Extended Security Update (ESU) program or those who have migrated to Exchange Server Subscription Edition able to meet it. Organizations may retain on-premises Exchange servers to support hybrid deployments, applications, or other systems that depend on them for email. According to Microsoft, throttling and blocking apply only to servers sending email to Exchange Online through an inbound OnPremises connector. Other delivery methods are unaffected, and the policy does not necessarily cover "all servers in your organization." The company added: "This might change in the future," which doesn't sound sinister at all. Administrators must balance the need to apply security patches promptly against the risk that an update introduces problems of its own – something Microsoft has, to put it delicately, caused in the past. Some administrators responding to the announcement were more than a little perturbed, although Microsoft has applied throttling and blocking to "significantly out of date" Exchange Server installations for several years. The announcement gives administrators advance notice of a change Microsoft says would have happened anyway. "With or without this announcement, the change would have been made (just as it was made in the past, without any announcement)," explained Microsoft Principal Project Manager Nino Bilic. "The bottom line is that admins really need to understand that updating Exchange should be done on time." ® Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? PostgreSQL 19 connects the dots with standardized graph queries
2026-09-04

PostgreSQL 19 connects the dots with standardized graph queries

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Baked-in SQL syntax arrives through multi-vendor collaboration, although indexing still needs to catch up PostgreSQL 19 is set to add standardized SQL syntax for querying property graphs. The open source database has steadily grown in popularity over the last five years, becoming the most popular among developers for the first time in 2023. Speaking to The Register, Alastair Turner, PostgreSQL evangelist at open source consultancy Percona, said the property graph query support in PostgreSQL 19 – due later this month or early October – resulted from collaboration among several of the database's largest contributors. Graph databases store data as nodes, relationships, and properties rather than tables or documents. They have advantages for certain workloads, but adopting one can mean adding another DBMS to the technology stack. The relational database has been acquiring graph query capabilities for some time, but PostgreSQL 19 is set to go further, Turner explained. "All the rest of it has sort of been done through Object Relational Mapping over the top. This is actually baked-in graph syntax. It comes from the SQL 2023 standard." Turner said the implementation was "very heavily influenced" by some of the engineers behind OpenCypher, the open source query language for graph databases. The work also involved PostgreSQL committers who sit on the SQL standards committee. "This is one of the most interesting developments because it's one of the big multi-vendor collaborations. There was lots of coding and review work across quite a few of the big contributors," he said. The new capability implements SQL/PGQ, which became part of the SQL standard in 2023. "It's on the right path," Turner said. "The SQL community and the PostgreSQL community are going to try to optimize everything." However, he added that the implementation "still needs one or two additional syntax features" before it can displace dedicated graph databases for a significant number of workloads. Turner said: "The current implementation creates queries across node-edge graphs to satisfy those requests. So some of them perform very well. What's going to have to happen in the next iteration or two is that indexing add-ons catch up to improve the performance." ® Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? JavaScript installer pnpm recast in Rust because ECMAScript can't keep up
2026-09-04

JavaScript installer pnpm recast in Rust because ECMAScript can't keep up

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Fully backward-compatible upgrade promises zero-friction speedup for monorepo wranglers. The popular JavaScript package manager pnpm, already known for its speedy downloads, has further cut installation times by up to 90 percent thanks to a complete rewrite in Rust. Yet the freshly released version 12 of pnpm should not cause consternation while upgrading, its maintainer says. “Upgrading should not feel like a migration. The commands, flags, settings, and lockfile format of pnpm 11 all carry over, and the documentation covers both versions,” wrote Zoltan Kochan, the current lead maintainer of pnpm. In the project’s own benchmarks, pnpm 12 installed a sample uncached package in 5.19 seconds, compared to the 8.22 seconds pnpm 11 required and the 47.7 seconds npm itself required. The project team also built a registry server in Rust, called pnpr, which improves performance even further. In the above test, the installation took only 3.37 seconds with pnpr on the backend. A new Rust core binary, on which work started in April, eliminates a lot of the overhead that comes from routing filesystem work through Node.js. This new version is 65.9 percent Rust and 33.5 percent TypeScript, according to the GitHub stats. “Package installation is dominated by fetching metadata and tarballs, unpacking files, resolving dependency graphs, and linking packages into node_modules, making it a natural target for native code and parallelism,” wrote Sarah Gooding, vice president of communications for security provider Socket, in a blog post. Gooding also noted that the project benchmarked pnpm against Bun and Yarn, but the results were removed due to testing SNAFUs. Nonetheless, she noted the software cut install times by up to 90 percent in some cases. Developer Rico Sta. Cruz originally created pnpm in 2016 as a faster alternative to npm (Node Package Manager), JavaScript’s de facto package manager, which devs used to find and download libraries and tools. GitHub acquired the open source npm software and repository in 2020. Npm garnered a reputation for being both sluggish and hungry for disk space, thanks to its generous practice of downloading all needed dependencies onto users’ machines, regardless of whether they were duplicates. Pnpm’s secret sauce is content-addressable storage. If you need a single library for 100 different applications, pnpm downloads it only once, and downloads another copy only if the developer specifies a different version. Like npm, pnpm still draws from the official npm repository, but arguably works better than npm for many. Many devs also like that the lockfile describing the package’s dependency tree was more readable thanks to its use of YAML. The 2025 State of JavaScript survey showed that pnpm was by far the most popular package manager for JavaScript monorepos, a party that also included Nx, Lerna, Yalc, Turborepo and even npm’s built-in Workspaces. About 38 percent (3,840) of 10,251 surveyed developers used the package manager. Rewriting applications in Rust seems to be a favorite activity these days, as the language is known for speedy performance unhampered by garbage collection. In the Web app ecosystem alone, the Bun framework just got an AI-fueled Rust makeover, Tailwind CSS version 4 got a new Rust engine called Oxide, and the Vite framework now speeds along thanks to a Rust-built JavaScript bundler called Rolldown. “It is a bit of [an] indictment on Node for having poor dx” (developer experience), wrote Darcy Clarke, CEO of JavaScript package management platform provider vlt, in an X message, about the Rust rewrite of pnpm. “It was faster to rewrite pnpm in rust than to migrate to esm” (ECMAScript modules), replied pnpm's Kochan, agreeing with Clarke’s sentiment. The software’s GitHub page still points at the last major release, pnpm 11.25, so existing users will have to update to the v12 branch specifically (“pnpm self-update next-12” will get you there). ® Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? The White House is making arcade games racist
2026-09-04

The White House is making arcade games racist

The Verge | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
The PR masterminds at the White House just released a series of vaguely policy-themed “arcade” games, some of which are racist — and modeled on real games whose copyright holders may not be too happy to be associated with the MAGA agenda. The Tetris Company has already responded to “Build the Wall,” which is obviously modeled on the famous puzzle game. In the Trump administration’s game, the player has to stack interlocking shapes to stop a “zombie border siege” along the southern border. Aliens — the administration’s preferred term for noncitizens, and the subject of previous grotesque attempts at humor — run across the screen, trying to get to America before the gates are shut. In a statement to The Verge, Tetris spokesperson Kerri Brusca clarified that the company wasn’t involved in the creation of Donald Trump’s game. “For more than 40 years, Tetris has brought people together across generations and cultures through play and joy. The objective of the game has always been to stack with purpose to clear as many lines as possible,” Brusca said. “We are currently reviewing the matter.” The various companies whose brands the White House ripped off to make and promote these games — including Sega, Nintendo, Xbox, and the Flappy Bird Foundation Group — did not immediately respond to The Verge’s request for comment. Previously, the Trump administration has gotten in trouble for infringing on others’ copyrights. The Department of Homeland Security has had to delete some of its racist, fancam-esque posts after receiving DMCA takedown requests. The purpose of Trump’s interminable memes, games, and other gimmicks is manifold: to drum up support for Trump’s policies, to entertain his groyper fans, and, of course, to troll the libs. (One game, “Rio Run,” is like snake, except the head of the snake is a Border Patrol agent who collects migrants as he moves across the screen. Ha ha, get it? The president is deporting people.) But the games also inadvertently highlight the administration’s policy failures, not to mention the hypocrisy of Trump’s agenda. Trump’s efforts to build a wall through protected lands in the Southwest, including Big Bend National Park, has been met with mass opposition — even from Republicans in deep-red Texas who form a key part of the MAGA coalition. Other games are similar reminders of how Trump’s policies are backfiring, even on the right. There’s “Flappy Bill,” a play on Flappy Bird that involves getting a bald eagle to deliver a piece of legislation to the National Mall. Cute! Except the administration has rolled back protections under the Endangered Species Act, the landmark legislation that helped rescue bald eagles from the brink of extinction. The administration’s supposed revamping of the National Mall has also been unpopular. In “Supply Line,” reminiscent of the game Tapper, you have to knock processed foods — those filled with additives, seed oils, and artificial colorings — off a conveyor belt before they get fed to schoolchildren. But Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. hasn’t managed to do any of that. Besides issuing a likely AI-drafted report for his Make America Healthy Again commission, RFK Jr.’s main role at HHS has been to roll back vaccine recommendations and food safety regulations. The administration’s gutting of the Food and Drug Administration made it harder to investigate the spread of cyclosporiasis, the so-called explosive diarrhea parasite, earlier this year. If anything, these games serve as a great reminder that our well-being is a big joke to the president and the people who surround him. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Roland is getting into generative AI music with Melody Flip
2026-09-04

Roland is getting into generative AI music with Melody Flip

The Verge | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Rather than fully polished tracks, the plugin is supposed to act as a creative spark. Rather than fully polished tracks, the plugin is supposed to act as a creative spark. It’s not quite the “push button; get song” of Suno, but Roland’s new Melody Flip tool marks the company’s foray into generative AI music. Available as a plug-in for your digital audio workstation (DAW), Melody Flip offers around 250 “Palettes,” which are essentially themed collections of musical ideas sorted by genre. You can start from scratch and ask it to generate any combination of melody, chord progression, bassline, or drums. Or you can feed it a reference track and have it build off the melodic ideas from that. Unlike Suno or Udio, Melody Flip doesn’t spit out fully polished songs with vocals and full arrangements. Instead, it offers simple musical loops meant to serve as a creative spark for you to build on. There are fairly limited control over the outputs. You can’t feed Melody Flip a text prompt like “acoustic country rock with clean male vocals, literate singer-songwriter, gentle brushed drums, and sparse pedal steel at 98BPM” and get a Jason Isbell ripoff. Instead, you basically just choose a genre, note density, BPM, musical key, and take what you get. Though the genre options are pretty fine-grained, going from standard fare like “80s disco” and “90s R&B” to esoteric things like “Kawaii Future Bass” and “Anime World.” The sound selection is further proof that you’re not supposed to use Melody Flip outputs out of the box. The presets available are mostly the sort of general MIDI tones you’d find in ‘90s video games. It seems like the intended use is to export the MIDI data from Melody Flip directly into your DAW to manipulate and pair with other, more powerful synth plugins. After years of seeming lost in the woods and alienating customers with its messy Roland Cloud subscription service, the company was back on track recently. The P-6 Creative Sampler, SH-4d, and Gaia 2 synth all addressed major issues with the company’s lineup. And Roland finally listened to customers last year, paying loving tribute to its analog heritage with the TR-1000. But considering the general response from the music community to generative AI, Melody Flip is unlikely to garner it much goodwill. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Microsoft says virtually nobody was grabbing NYT articles through its chatbot
2026-09-04

Microsoft says virtually nobody was grabbing NYT articles through its chatbot

The Verge | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Fewer than 1 percent of more than 8 million chat logs regurgitated at least 16 words. Fewer than 1 percent of more than 8 million chat logs regurgitated at least 16 words. Microsoft’s Copilot rarely reproduces even full sentences from news articles and books, let alone substantive chunks that could substitute for the original, the company says in new legal filings as it fights copyright claims from publishers including The New York Times and book authors. As part of the lawsuit’s discovery, Microsoft provided 8.2 million Copilot chat logs to an expert hired by news publishers. The logs, it claims, were specifically chosen “because they hit on keywords implicating use of News Plaintiffs’ websites, and therefore the most likely to contain News Plaintiffs’ works.” It says the resulting analysis shows that 59,545 of these contained at least 16 words in common with news content used to ground the AI model. An expert for the Center for Investigative Reporting found 51 instances of “substantial overlap” with CIR work in the dataset, Microsoft says. Similarly, an expert in the authors’ suit found that the 8.2 million conversations with Copilot only had 24 responses that contained at least 30 matching words. Only 10 of the 212 books evaluated had any matches, Microsoft claims. The Times disagreed with Microsoft’s conclusions. “The documents and testimony uncovered during discovery lead to only one conclusion: Microsoft and OpenAI stole from The New York Times to make commercial products that substitute for its journalism, threaten its business, and undermine its industry,” the Times’ lead counsel Ian Crosby said in a statement. “We look forward to Microsoft and OpenAI being held accountable for their theft.” CIR, and Authors Guild did not immediately respond to requests for comment. Microsoft argues that the numbers bolster its case that using copyrighted content for AI training datasets should be considered fair use. While systems like Copilot rely on using copyrighted material, it says, the resulting systems are used for significantly different purposes than the original. The fact that they sometimes reproduce sections of text, it concludes, “hardly undermines the transformative purpose of LLM training.” The filings were made as part of a legal battle brought by publishers and authors against Microsoft and OpenAI, which the plaintiffs claim built products on their works and now compete with them directly, in part by regurgitating copyrighted content. The news publishers’ and books authors’ claims were consolidated under one judge to streamline the process, despite objections from the publishers and authors. Microsoft submitted its filing on Friday as it argues for the judge to issue a summary judgement, which would end the case at an early stage. (The Trump administration also filed a statement of interest in the New York Times case this week, supporting OpenAI.) If the judge sides with the publishers and authors, the legal battle will continue in court. A free daily digest of the news that matters most.
